, we use these to study spanning manifolds of codimension 2 embeddings and prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.3
.

??
is primarily a review of the relative normal Euler number of immersed submanifolds.

We then specialise to the case of surfaces in 4-manifolds.
In
??
, we study spanning manifolds of properly embedded surfaces given by puncturing immersions around double points.
In
??
, we prove Theorems
D
and
E
, and Corollary
F
.

??
is primarily a review of facts about ambient surgery featured in
[
30
]
and
[
21
]
.
The theory of
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery is developed in
??
. In
??
, we prove Theorem
C
.

2.
Representing homology classes by properly embedded submanifolds

For spaces
A
and
B
, we write
[A,B]
for the set of continuous maps
A\to B
up to homotopy.
It is a standard result in algebraic topology that for any abelian group
G
and any
n\geq 1
, there is a fundamental class
\alpha\in H^{n}(K(G,n);G)
such that for any CW-complex
X
, the map
[X,K(G,n)]\to H^{n}(X;G)
given by
[f]\mapsto f^{*}\alpha
is a bijection
[
32
, Chapter V.4]
. One consequence of this and Poincaré duality, originally due to Thom, is that all
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology classes of codimension 1 in closed manifolds are represented by embedded closed manifolds. Similarly, all
\mathbb{Z}
-homology classes of codimension 1 or 2 in closed oriented manifolds are represented by embedded closed oriented manifolds
[
31
, §II.11]
.

In this section, we prove generalisations of these realisation results arbitrary manifolds, possibly non-compact and with non-empty boundary. In particular, we show in
??
that the submanifolds representing given relative homology classes of small enough codimension can be taken to have any prescribed boundary satisfying the necessary homological conditions. We will use these results to prove the existence of spanning manifolds (see
??
) and oriented cobordisms (see
??
) with prescribed boundaries.

2.1.
Homotopy with compact support

For a space
A
and a pointed space
(B,*)
, let
\Map_{c}(A,B)
be the maps
A\to B
with compact support, i.e.

\Map_{c}(A,B)\coloneq\big\{f\colon A\to B\mid\exists K\subseteq A\text{ compact s.t. }f(A\setminus K)\subseteq\{*\}\big\}.

For
f,g\in\Map_{c}(A,B)
, we write
f\sim_{c}g
if there is a homotopy from
f
to
g
with compact support; that is, if there is a compact set
K\subseteq A
such that
f\simeq g
rel.
A\setminus K
and
f(A\setminus K)=g(A\setminus K)\subseteq\{*\}
.
Then
\sim_{c}
is an equivalence relation on
\Map_{c}(A,B)
, and we write

[A,B]_{c}\coloneq\frac{\Map_{c}(A,B)}{\sim_{c}}

for the set of equivalence classes.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

Although both
\Map_{c}(A,B)
and
[A,B]_{c}
do in general depend on the choice of basepoint
*\in B
, this dependence is trivial when
B
is a connected manifold. This is the only case that we will consider, so we suppress basepoint-dependence in our notation. If
A
is compact, then
\Map_{c}(A,B)=\Map(A,B)
and
[A,B]_{c}=[A,B]
as would be expected.

The set
[A,B]_{c}
also admits a description as a colimit of sets.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

For any space
A
and pointed space
(B,*)
,

[A,B]_{c}=\colim_{K\subseteq A}[(A,A\setminus K),(B,*)],

where the colimit is taken over all compact subsets
K\subseteq A
ordered by inclusion.

Proof.

By the construction of a colimit of sets, an element of
\colim[(A,A\setminus K),(B,*)]
is represented by a homotopy class of maps
[f]\in[(A,A\setminus K),(B,*)]
for some
K\subseteq A
compact. Then
[f]
represents the same class in the colimit as
[g]\in[(A,A\setminus K^{\prime}),(B,*)]
if and only if there is a compact subset
K^{\prime\prime}\supseteq K\cup K^{\prime}
such that

[f]=[g]\in[(A,A\setminus K^{\prime\prime}),(B,*)].

But this is exactly saying that there is a homotopy from
f
to
g
with compact support.
∎

We now give our bijection between homology classes rel. boundary and compactly supported homotopy classes of maps to Eilenberg–MacLane spaces. This is mostly a formal consequence of
??
and the Poincaré duality between homology rel. boundary and cohomology with compact support.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
M
be an
n
-manifold.

(i)

Let
k\geq n
and fix a basepoint
*\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k}
. There is a bijection

[M,\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})

given by
[f]\mapsto[f^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k})]
for any representative
f
such that
f\pitchfork\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k}
.

Now suppose that
M
is oriented.

(ii)

Fix a basepoint
*\in S^{1}\setminus\{1\}
. There is a bijection

[M,S^{1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})

given by
[f]\mapsto[f^{-1}(\{1\})]
for any representative
f
such that
f\pitchfork\{1\}
, i.e. such that
1
is a regular value of
f
.

(iii)

Let
k\geq\left\lfloor(n-1)/2\right\rfloor
and fix a basepoint
*\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}\setminus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k}
. There is a bijection

[M,\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})

given by
[f]\mapsto[f^{-1}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k})]
for any representative
f
such that
f\pitchfork\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k}
.

Proof.

We prove (i). The arguments for (ii) and (iii) are almost identical, using
S^{1}
as a
K(\mathbb{Z},1)
-space and
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
as a
K(\mathbb{Z},2)
-space.

By
??
and cellular approximation, we get bijections

[M,\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}]_{c}=\colim_{K\subseteq M}[(M,M\setminus K),(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k+1},*)]\xrightarrow{\cong}\colim_{K\subseteq M}[(M,M\setminus K),(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty},*)],

where both colimits are colimits of sets taken over compact subsets
K\subseteq M
.
Since
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
is a
K({\mathbb{Z}/{2}},1)
-space, there is a bijection

\colim_{K\subseteq M}[(M,M\setminus K),(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty},*)]\xrightarrow{\cong}\colim_{K\subseteq M}H^{1}(M,M\setminus K;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})=H^{1}_{c}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),

where
H^{*}_{c}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
is cohomology with compact support. By the non-compact version of Poincaré duality
[
28
, §6]
, we get a bijection

\PD\colon H^{1}_{c}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

Composing all of the above, we get the required bijection.

To see the explicit form, note that the fundamental class
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
is the generator, and that the restriction
\alpha^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k+1};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
is Poincaré dual to the generator
[\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k}]\in H_{n}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k+1};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
. Then since transverse preimages are Poincaré dual to pullbacks (see e.g.
[
3
, Theorem 11.16]
),

\PD(f^{*}\alpha^{\prime})=[f^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k})]\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),

whenever
f\pitchfork\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{k}
. Finally, recall that such
f
are generic
[
12
, IV§4]
.
∎

Remark
\thetheorem
.

The assumption that
M
is oriented in (ii) and (iii) is not in fact required. In general, the proof gives bijections

[M,S^{1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}})\quad\text{and}\quad[M,\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}}),

where
w_{1}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to\Aut(\mathbb{Z})
is the orientation character of
M
.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

??
also holds in the topological category, since all transversality statements also hold for topological transversality. See the proof of Theorem 10.11 of
[
11
]
for the case of
M
oriented and compact; the same modifications as given in the proof above for
M
unoriented and non-compact go through in that setting. In turn, this implies that all later results in this section as well as all direct applications of them also go through in the topological category without any major modifications. We therefore omit any future remarks on proofs in the topological category.

2.2.
Representing homology classes with prescribed boundaries

We can use
??
to represent relative homology classes by compact embedded manifolds with prescribed boundaries. Let
M
be an
n
-manifold and let
A\subseteq\partial M
be an embedded
(n-1)
-manifold which is closed as a subspace of
M
. Define the maps on homology (with any choices of coefficients and degree)

\partial_{A}\colon H_{*}(M,\partial M)\xrightarrow{\partial}H_{*-1}(\partial M,\partial M\setminus A)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{*-1}(A,\partial A),

where the first map is the boundary map in the long exact sequence of the triple
(M,\partial M,\partial M\setminus A)
, and the second is the inverse of an excision isomorphism. This is given explicitly on a relative cycle
\sigma
in
(M,\partial M)
by
\partial_{A}[\sigma]=[\sigma\cap A]
.

We can use
??
(i) to represent any
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology class of codimension 1 by an unoriented submanifold with appropriate prescribed boundary. The methods we use here to control the boundary of the representing submanifolds are adapted and expanded from
[
1
]
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
B\subset A
be a properly embedded compact
(n-2)
-manifold.
For any
\beta\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
with
\partial_{A}\beta=[B]\in H_{n-2}(A,\partial A;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
,
there exists a properly embedded compact
(n-1)
-manifold
Y\subset M
such that
Y\cap A=B
and
[Y]=\beta\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

Proof.

Fix a basepoint
*\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Let
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
be a representative of the class corresponding to
\beta
under the bijection

[M,\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})

in
??
(i). We may assume that
f\pitchfork\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}
, so that
Y\coloneq f^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n})\subset M

is a properly embedded compact
(n-1)
-manifold with
[Y]=\beta\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

We now show that if there exists some map
g\colon A\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
with compact support such that
g^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n})=B
, then we can choose
f
so that
Y\cap A=\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{A}}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n})=B
.
Indeed, suppose that such a
g
exists. Since both
B
and
Y\cap A
represent the class
\partial_{A}\beta\in H_{n-2}(A,\partial A;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, they are homologous in
A
rel.
\partial A
. Then by applying
??
(i) to
A
with
k=n
, we see that
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{A}}\simeq_{c}g
. That is, there is a homotopy
H\colon A\times I\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
from
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{A}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{A}}
to
g
, and a compact set
K\subseteq A
such that
H\big((A\setminus K)\times I\big)\subseteq\{*\}
. Let
N\subseteq M
be a compact neighbourhood of
K
. Then
H
extends to a homotopy
H^{\prime}\colon(A\cup\overline{M\setminus N})\times I\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
by

H^{\prime}(x,t)=\begin{cases}H(x,t)&x\in A,\\
*&x\in\overline{M\setminus N}.\end{cases}

So
H^{\prime}
is a homotopy from
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}
to
g\cup\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}
.
Since
A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}
is a closed subspace of
M
, the inclusion
A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}\hookrightarrow M
is a cofibration
[
3
, Chapter VII.1]
, and hence
H^{\prime}
can be extended over
M
to a homotopy from
f
to some
f^{\prime}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
with

\mathchoice{{f^{\prime}\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{14.2678pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{13.40672pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f^{\prime}\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.7678pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.90672pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f^{\prime}\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{11.46223pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{10.60115pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f^{\prime}\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{9.49998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{8.6389pt}}_{A\cup\overline{M\setminus N}}}=g\cup\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{\overline{M\setminus N}}}.

This homotopy is supported in
N
, so has compact support. Hence
f\simeq_{c}f^{\prime}
. So by replacing
f
by
f^{\prime}
, we may assume that
Y\cap A=\mathchoice{{f^{\prime}\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{14.2678pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{13.40672pt}}_{A}}{{f^{\prime}\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.7678pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.90672pt}}_{A}}{{f^{\prime}\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{11.46223pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{10.60115pt}}_{A}}{{f^{\prime}\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{9.49998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{8.6389pt}}_{A}}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n})=B
, and the result follows.

It remains to construct such a map
g\colon A\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
. Let
\nu B\subset A
be a tubular neighbourhood for
B
in
A
, and hence a
D^{1}
-bundle over
B
. The universal
D^{1}
-bundle is the disc bundle of the tautological line bundle
\gamma\colon E\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
, so after cellular approximation we obtain a bundle morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nu B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{DE^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\gamma^{n}}

where
\gamma^{n}\colon E^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}
is the tautological line bundle
[
24
, §5.1]
. Since the Thom space
{DE^{n}/SE^{n}}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
, we can extend
g_{0}
to a map

g\colon A\xrightarrow{\text{collapse}}\nu B/S\nu B\xrightarrow{\overline{g_{0}}}DE^{n}/SE^{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}.

This map satisfies
g^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n})=B
by construction, so
g
is the required map. In the case that
A
is non-compact, we must ensure that the chosen basepoint is the point
g(A\setminus\nu B)
. This can be arranged by choosing an appropriate diffeomorphism
DE^{n}/SE^{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
.
∎

Using
??
(ii), we can show an analogous result when all manifolds are taken to be oriented and homology is taken with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
M
and
A
are oriented. Let
B\subset A
be an oriented properly embedded compact
(n-2)
-manifold.
For any
\beta\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
with
\partial_{A}\beta=[B]\in H_{n-2}(A,\partial A;\mathbb{Z})
,
there exists an oriented properly embedded compact
(n-1)
-manifold
Y\subset M
such that
Y\cap A=B
and
[Y]=\beta\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of
??
. Let
f\colon M\to S^{1}
be a representative of the class corresponding to
\beta
under the bijection

[M,S^{1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})

in
??
(ii), and assume
1
is a regular value of
f
. Then
Y\coloneq f^{-1}(\{1\})\subset M
is a oriented properly embedded compact manifold with
[Y]=\beta\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
.
As in the unoriented case, to arrange that
Y\cap A=B
, it suffices to show that there is a map
g\colon A\to S^{1}
with compact support such that
g^{-1}(\{1\})=B
.

To construct the map
g\colon A\to S^{1}
, let
\nu B\subset A
be a tubular neighbourhood of
B
. Then since
A
and
B
are oriented,
\nu B
is the trivial
D^{1}
-bundle over
B
. Hence there is projection map
\nu B\to D^{1}
, and the Thom construction gives the required map

g\colon A\xrightarrow{\text{collapse}}\nu B/S\nu B\to D^{1}/\partial D^{1}\cong S^{1}

with
g^{-1}(\{1\})=B
.
∎

By
??
(iii), the same construction works for
\mathbb{Z}
-homology classes of codimension 2.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
M
and
A
are oriented. Let
B\subset A
an oriented properly embedded compact
(n-3)
-manifold.
Then for any
\beta\in H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
with
\partial_{A}\beta=[B]\in H_{n-3}(A,\partial A;\mathbb{Z})
,
there exists an oriented properly embedded compact
(n-2)
-manifold
Y\subset M
such that
Y\cap A=B
and
[Y]=\beta\in H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Let
k=\left\lfloor(n-1)/2\right\rfloor
, and let
f\colon M\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}
be a representative of the class corresponding to
\beta
under the bijection

[M,\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}]_{c}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})

in
??
(iii), and assume
f\pitchfork\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k}
. Then
Y\coloneq f^{-1}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k})\subset M
is a oriented properly embedded compact manifold with
[Y]=\beta\in H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
.
As before, to arrange that
Y\cap A=B
, it suffices to show that there is a map
g\colon A\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}
with compact support such that
g^{-1}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k})=B
.

The construction of such a map is nearly identical to the unoriented codimension 1 case. Let
\nu B\subset A
be a tubular neighbourhood, which is an oriented
D^{2}
-bundle over
B
since
A
and
B
are oriented.
The universal oriented
D^{2}
-bundle is the disc bundle of the tautological oriented rank 2 bundle
\gamma\colon E\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
, so after cellular approximation,
\nu B
is classified by a bundle morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nu B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{DE^{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\gamma^{k}}

where
\gamma^{k}\colon E^{k}\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k}
is the tautological oriented rank 2 bundle. The Thom space
DE^{k}/SE^{k}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1}
, so the Thom construction gives the required map

g\colon A\xrightarrow{\text{collapse}}\nu B/S\nu B\xrightarrow{\overline{g_{0}}}DE^{k}/SE^{k}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k+1},

with
g^{-1}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k})=B
.
∎

There is no complete analogue to
??
allowing us to represent codimension 2 classes in
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology by properly embedded compact submanifolds. However we can make some progress in the case of
n=4
; see e.g. Chapter 2 of
[
19
]
or Remark 1.2.4 of
[
13
]
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
X
be a 4-manifold, and fix
\alpha\in H_{2}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Then there exists a properly embedded compact surface
\Sigma\subset X
such that
[\Sigma]=\alpha
.

The same amount of control over the boundary as in
??
can also be obtained in this case, although this uses different techniques and will not be necessary for our applications.

3.
Spanning manifolds of codimension 2 embeddings

This section contains a discussion of spanning manifolds of codimension 2 proper embeddings. Recall that if
X
is an
(n+2)
-manifold and
\Sigma\subset X
is a properly embedded compact
n
-manifold, then a spanning manifold for
\Sigma
is a compact
(n+1)
-manifold
Y
with corners, embedded in
X
, such that
\partial Y=\Sigma\cup Z
, where
Z\subset\partial X
is an embedded
n
-manifold.

In
??
, we define the notion of a Seifert section of a codimension 2 properly embedded submanifold
\Sigma\subset X
. This is a section of the normal circle bundle
SN_{X}\Sigma
, which is a generalisation of a Seifert framing of a knot or link in
S^{3}
.
In
??
, we give exact conditions for an oriented proper codimension 2 embedding in an oriented manifold to admit a spanning manifold.
In
??
, we show that an unoriented properly embedded submanifold admits a spanning manifold if and only if its normal circle bundle admits a Seifert section.
Sections
3.4
and
3.5
, prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.3
by giving sufficient conditions for the existence of a Seifert section.

Notation
\thetheorem
.

For the rest of this section, fix an
(n+2)
-manifold
X
and a properly embedded compact
n
-manifold
\Sigma\subset X
, with
n\geq 1
.
Fix a tubular neighbourhood
(\nu\Sigma,\varphi)
for
\Sigma
, and recall that we write

S\nu\Sigma\coloneq\varphi(SN_{X}\Sigma)\subseteq\partial(\nu\Sigma)

for the embedding of the normal circle bundle around
\Sigma
.
Write
\pi\colon S\nu\Sigma\to\Sigma
for the
S^{1}
-bundle structure such that
\mathchoice{{\pi\varphi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{10.55553pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{9.69444pt}}_{SN_{X}\Sigma}}{{\pi\varphi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{10.55553pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{9.69444pt}}_{SN_{X}\Sigma}}{{\pi\varphi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{8.68053pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{7.81944pt}}_{SN_{X}\Sigma}}{{\pi\varphi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{7.43053pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{6.56944pt}}_{SN_{X}\Sigma}}\colon SN_{X}\Sigma\to\Sigma
is the usual projection map.
Let
E\coloneq\overline{X\setminus\nu\Sigma}\subset X
be the exterior of
\Sigma
. Note that
E
is a manifold with corners, and that

\partial E=(\partial X\cap\partial E)\mathop{\cup}_{\partial(S\nu\Sigma)}S\nu\Sigma.

We also define the maps on homology (with any choice of coefficient ring)

\partial_{S}\colon H_{n+1}(E,\partial E)\xrightarrow{\partial}H_{n}(\partial E,\partial X\cap\partial E)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma))

and

\partial_{X}\colon H_{n+1}(E,\partial E)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n+1}(X,\partial X\cup\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\partial}H_{n}(\partial X\cup\Sigma,\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}(\partial X,\partial\Sigma),

where all marked isomorphisms are excision isomorphisms or their inverses, and all indicated boundary maps come from the appropriate long exact sequences of triples. If
\sigma
is a relative
(n+1)
-cycle in
(E,\partial E)
, then
\partial_{S}[\sigma]=[\partial\sigma\cap S\nu\Sigma]
and
\partial_{X}[\sigma]=[\partial\sigma\cap\partial X]
.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

If
\partial_{0}X\subseteq\partial X
is a boundary component disjoint from
\Sigma
, then we may replace the ambient manifold
X
with
X\setminus\partial_{0}X
. In this way, we can assume that all constructions occur away from boundary components of
X
that do not meet
\Sigma
. In particular, if
\Sigma
is closed, then all results in this section still hold when considering
H_{n}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
instead of
H_{n}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and assuming that all constructions occur in the interior of
X
.

3.1.
Seifert sections

Suppose that
\Sigma
has a spanning manifold
Y
.
By taking the tubular neighbourhood
\nu\Sigma
small enough, we may assume that
Y\cap\nu\Sigma
is a collar of
\Sigma
in
Y
. Then
Y\cap S\nu\Sigma
is a push-off of
\Sigma
, and hence is the image of a section
\Sigma\to S\nu\Sigma
of
\pi
. In this way,
Y
determines a section
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
via
\varphi
, given by the direction into
Y
. This is analogous to the Seifert framing of a knot in
S^{3}
, though in the general case the normal bundle
N_{X}\Sigma
need not be trivial. We formalise this notion as follows, and refer the reader to
??
.

X
Y
\Sigma
\Sigma

Figure 3.
A proper codimension 2 embedding with
X=D^{3}
and
\Sigma=I\sqcup I
(red). A spanning surface
Y
(yellow) is given. The push-off
\Sigma^{s}_{+}
of
\Sigma
determined by the Seifert section associated to
Y
is shown (dashed red).

Definition
\thetheorem
.

Let
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
be a section. We write
\Sigma^{s}_{+}\coloneq\varphi s(\Sigma)\subset S\nu\Sigma
for the push-off of
\Sigma
in the direction of
s
.
We call
s
a Seifert section if

[\Sigma^{s}_{+}]=(\varphi s)_{*}[\Sigma]\in\im\partial_{S}\subseteq H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

Given a spanning manifold
Y
for
\Sigma
, suppose that
\nu\Sigma
is small enough that
Y\cap\nu\Sigma
is a collar neighbourhood for
\Sigma
in
Y
. The Seifert section associated to
Y
is the unique section
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
such that
\Sigma^{s}_{+}=Y\cap S\nu\Sigma
.

Note that whether a section of
SN_{X}\Sigma
is a Seifert section or not is independent of the choice of tubular neighbourhood
(\nu\Sigma,\varphi)
. Moreover, the choice of tubular neighbourhood only affects the Seifert section associated to spanning manifold
Y
by an isotopy. If
Z\subset\partial X
is a spanning manifold for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
, and
Z
extends to a spanning manifold
Y
for
\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s
, then
s^{Z}=\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
after identifying
SN_{\partial X}{\partial\Sigma}=\mathchoice{{SN_{X}\Sigma\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{SN_{X}\Sigma\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{SN_{X}\Sigma\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.09444pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.23335pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{SN_{X}\Sigma\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.41666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.55557pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

It is interesting and important to remark that
SN_{X}\Sigma
can admit a Seifert section only if
\Sigma
is null-homologous. To see this, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n+1}(X,\partial X\cup\Sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(\partial X\cup\Sigma,\partial X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(X,\partial X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n+1}(E,\partial E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(X,\partial X),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\partial_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

where marked isomorphisms are excision isomorphisms, unlabelled arrows are induced by inclusion, and
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-coefficients are omitted.
The top row is exact by the long exact sequence of the triple
(X,\partial X\cup\Sigma,\partial X)
; the left square commutes by the definition of
\partial_{S}
; the right square commutes since all maps are induced by inclusion. Hence the bottom row is exact, and if
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
is a Seifert section, then

[\Sigma]=\pi_{*}[\Sigma^{s}_{+}]\in\im\big(H_{n}(E,\partial E)\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}\partial_{S}}H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)\big)=\ker\big(H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)\to H_{n}(X,\partial X)\big),

and so
[\Sigma]
is null-homologous.

3.2.
Existence of oriented spanning manifolds

We first prove an oriented version of Theorem
1.3
; that is, we prove that a codimension 2 proper embedding admits a spanning manifold if and only if it is null-homotopic and has a trivial normal bundle. We feel this is instructive even though it is very similar to standard results in e.g. Chapter VIII of
[
19
]
, since the proof follows many of the the same steps as the proof of Theorems
1.3
and
1.3
. Recall that we call
Y
an oriented spanning manifold for
\Sigma
extending
Z\subset\partial X
if
Y
and
Z
are oriented, and
\partial Y=\Sigma\cup Z
as oriented manifolds.

The proof strategy, both in the oriented and unoriented case, is as follows. First, impose assumptions on the normal circle bundle
SN_{X}\Sigma
that guarantee that it admits a Seifert section
s
. Then consider the inclusions
\Sigma^{s}_{+}\subset S\nu\Sigma\subseteq\partial E
. By applying
??
(or in the unoriented case,
??
) with
M=E
,
A=S\nu\Sigma
, and
B=\Sigma^{s}_{+}
, we find a spanning manifold
\widehat{Y}\subset E
for
\Sigma^{s}_{+}
which is contained in the exterior of
\Sigma
. This can then be extended linearly through
\nu\Sigma
to a spanning manifold
Y
for
\Sigma
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
X
and
\Sigma
are oriented. Then
\Sigma
admits an oriented spanning manifold if and only if
N_{X}\Sigma
is trivial and
[\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
.

If
X
is closed, it is enough to assume that
[\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, since this implies that
N_{X}\Sigma
is trivial
[
19
, Theorem VIII.2]
.

Proof.

We first prove the forward direction, so suppose that
Y
is an oriented spanning manifold for
\Sigma
. Then
\Sigma
must be null-homologous, and
SN_{X}\Sigma
must admit a Seifert section, namely the Seifert section associated to
Y
. Since
SN_{X}\Sigma
is an oriented
S^{1}
-bundle over an oriented base which admits a section, it must be trivial. Then
N_{X}\Sigma
is also trivial, proving the forward direction.

For the reverse direction, we use the argument outlined above. Suppose that
N_{X}\Sigma
is trivial and that
[\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
. Repeating the argument in
??
with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients, exactness of the bottom row shows that there is a class
\beta\in H_{n+1}(E,\partial E;\mathbb{Z})
such that

\pi_{*}\partial_{S}\beta=[\Sigma]\in H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}).

Since
N_{X}\Sigma
is trivial, so is
SN_{X}\Sigma
. Thus for each class
\alpha\in H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);\mathbb{Z})
such that
\pi_{*}\alpha=[\Sigma]
, there is a section
s\colon\Sigma\to S\nu\Sigma
of
\pi
such that
s_{*}[\Sigma]=\alpha
. In particular, there is a Seifert section
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
such that

[\Sigma_{+}^{s}]=\partial_{S}\beta\in H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);\mathbb{Z}).

After smoothing the corners of
E
, we can apply
??
with
M=E
,
A=S\nu\Sigma
, and
B=\Sigma_{+}^{s}
to find a properly embedded compact
(n+1)
-manifold
\widehat{Y}\subset E
such that
\widehat{Y}\cap S\nu\Sigma=\Sigma_{+}^{s}
and
[\widehat{Y}]=\beta\in H_{n+1}(E,\partial E;\mathbb{Z})
. Finally, we can extend
\widehat{Y}
through
\nu\Sigma
by setting

Y\coloneq\varphi\big(\{\lambda\cdot s(x)\mid\lambda\in[0,1],\,x\in\Sigma\}\big)\cup\widehat{Y}\subset X.

Then
Y
is an oriented spanning manifold for
\Sigma
as required.
∎

We can similarly prove an oriented version of
??
, classifying when an oriented spanning manifold for
\partial\Sigma
extends to one of
\Sigma
. Recall that we say a spanning manifold
Z\subset\partial X
for
\partial\Sigma
extends to a spanning manifold
Y\subset X
for
\Sigma
if
\partial Y=\Sigma\cup Z
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
X
and
\Sigma
are oriented. Let
Z\subset\partial X
be an oriented spanning manifold for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
. Then
\Sigma
admits an oriented spanning manifold extending
Z
if and only if
N_{X}\Sigma
is trivial and
[\Sigma\cup Z]=0\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The forward direction follows quickly by the same argument as in
??
. So we prove the reverse. If
[\Sigma\cup Z]=0\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, then there is an
(n+1)
-chain
\beta
in
X
with boundary
\partial\beta=\Sigma\cup Z
. Then
\beta
represents a class

[\beta]\in H_{n+1}(E,\partial E;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{n+1}(X,\partial X\cup\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),

where the isomorphism follows by excision.
So again
\pi_{*}\partial_{S}[\beta]=[\Sigma]\in H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. Since
SN_{X}\Sigma
is trivial, there is a section
s
of
SN_{X}\Sigma
such that
[\Sigma_{+}^{s}]=\partial_{S}[\beta]
.

Since both
Z\cap E
and
\Sigma^{s}_{+}
meet
\partial(S\nu\Sigma)
transversely, we can smooth the corners of
E
so that

B\coloneq(Z\cap E)\cup\Sigma_{+}^{s}\subset\partial E

is a smoothly embedded submanifold. Note that
\partial[\beta]=[B]\in H_{n}(\partial E;\mathbb{Z})
. We can thus apply
??
with
M=E
and
A=\partial E
to find a properly embedded compact
(n+1)
-manifold
\widehat{Y}\subset E
such that
\widehat{Y}\cap\partial E=\Sigma_{+}^{s}\cup Z
.
As
\widehat{Y}\cap S\nu\Sigma=\Sigma^{s}_{+}
is the image of a section of
\pi\colon S\nu\Sigma\to\Sigma
, we may extend
\widehat{Y}
through
\nu\Sigma
to obtain a spanning manifold
Y\subset X
for
\Sigma
as in the proof of
??
.
Then
Y\cap\partial X
is at least isotopic to
Z
rel.
\partial\Sigma
, so we can perform an isotopy to arrange that
Y
extends
Z
.
∎

3.3.
Existence of a spanning manifold given a Seifert section

We now return to the unoriented situation.
In this subsection, we use the proof strategy outlined in
??
to show that every Seifert section of
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
arises as the Seifert section associated to some spanning manifold. We do this in two steps. The first step is to show that if the restriction
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
is the Seifert section associated to some spanning manifold of
\partial\Sigma\subset\partial X
, then
s
is the Seifert section associated to some spanning manifold of
\Sigma
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
be a Seifert section and let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning manifold for
\partial\Sigma
whose associated Seifert section is
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
. Then
Z
extends to a spanning manifold
Y
for
\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s
if and only if

[(Z\cap E)\cup\Sigma^{s}_{+}]=0\in H_{n}(E;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

In this case, for any
\beta\in H_{n+1}(E,\partial E;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
such that
\partial_{S}\beta=[\Sigma^{s}_{+}]
and
\partial_{X}\beta=[Z]
, we can choose
Y
such that
[Y\cap E]=\beta
.

Proof.

Write
B\coloneq(Z\cap E)\cup\Sigma^{s}_{+}
. The forward direction follows quickly, since
B=\partial(Y\cap E)
, so must be null-homologous in
E
.

We now prove the reverse direction. Suppose that
[B]=0\in H_{n}(E;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, so that there exists some
(n+1)
-chain in
E
with boundary
B
. In particular, we can find some
\beta\in H_{n+1}(E,\partial E;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
such that
\partial_{S}\beta=[\Sigma_{+}^{s}]
and
\partial_{X}\beta=[Z]
. After smoothing the corners of
E
so that
B\subset\partial E
is a smoothly embedded submanifold, we apply
??
with
M=E
and
A=\partial E
to find a compact properly embedded
(n+1)
-manifold
\widehat{Y}\subset E
with
\widehat{Y}\cap A=B
and
[\widehat{Y}]=\beta\in H_{n+1}(E,\partial E;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

As
\widehat{Y}\cap S\nu\Sigma=\Sigma^{s}_{+}
is the image of a section of
\pi\colon S\nu\Sigma\to\Sigma
, we may extend
\widehat{Y}
through
\nu\Sigma
to obtain a spanning manifold
Y\subset X
for
\Sigma
as in the proof of
??
.
Then
Y\cap\partial X
is at least isotopic to
Z
rel.
\partial\Sigma
, so we can perform an isotopy to arrange that
Y
extends
Z
.
∎

We can now apply
??
to
\partial\Sigma\subset\partial X
, and use the fact that
\partial X
has empty boundary to argue that
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
must always be the Seifert section associated to some spanning manifold of
\partial\Sigma
. Hence any Seifert section
s
is associated to some spanning manifold for
\Sigma
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
be a Seifert section. Then
\Sigma
admits a spanning manifold
Y
whose associated Seifert section is
s
.

Proof.

Since
s
is a Seifert section, we can find an
n
-chain
\alpha
in
\partial X\cap\partial E
and an
(n+1)
-chain
\beta
in
E
such that
\partial\beta=\alpha\cup\Sigma^{s}_{+}
and
\partial\alpha=\partial\Sigma^{s}_{+}
. Since
\partial X
has empty boundary, we can apply
??
with
\partial\Sigma\subset\partial X
in place of
\Sigma\subset X
and
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
in place of
s
. This gives a spanning manifold
Z\subset\partial X
for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
and such that
[Z]=[\alpha]\in H_{n}(\partial X,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Then

[(Z\cap E)\cup\Sigma^{s}_{+}]=[\alpha\cup\Sigma^{s}_{+}]=[\partial\beta]=0\in H_{n}(E;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

Finally we can apply
??
to
\Sigma
,
s
, and
Z
, to find a spanning manifold for
\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s
.
∎

Corollary
\thetheorem
.

The submanifold
\Sigma\subset X
admits a spanning manifold if and only if
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a Seifert section.

Hence the question of whether
\Sigma
admits a spanning manifold is equivalent to the question of whether
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a Seifert section. The next section will focus on giving sufficient conditions for all possible
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology classes of the total space of an arbitrary
S^{1}
-bundle to represented by the images of sections, which will give sufficient (but not necessary) conditions for
\Sigma
to admit a spanning manifold.

3.4.
Existence of Seifert sections

We now consider sufficient conditions for
SN_{X}\Sigma
to admit a Seifert section, and prove
??
. As discussed in
??
, when
X
and
\Sigma
are orientable,
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a Seifert section if and only if
\Sigma
is null-homologous and
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits any section at all, since this implies that
SN_{X}\Sigma
is trivial. This is not true without the assumption on the orientability of
X
and
\Sigma
. Instead, we will have to place other assumptions on the inclusion
\Sigma\subset X
.

We recall some facts about homology with local coefficients, and refer the reader to Chapter VI of
[
32
]
for further details. Let
B
be a compact
n
-manifold.
Any group homomorphism

w\colon\pi_{1}(B)\to\Aut(\mathbb{Z})\cong\{\pm 1\}

defines a left
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
-module with underlying abelian group
\mathbb{Z}
and the action of
\pi_{1}(B)
given by
w
, which we write
\mathbb{Z}^{w}
. This in turn defines a local coefficient system for
B
.
The unique non-zero homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\to{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
-modules given by reduction mod 2 induces change-of-coefficient homomorphisms

\rho\colon H^{*}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\to H^{*}(B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

If
\xi\colon M\to B
is a vector bundle or sphere bundle, we write
w_{1}(\xi)\colon\pi_{1}(B)\to\{\pm 1\}
for the orientation character of
\xi
. Explicitly, for a loop
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to B
, this is given by
w_{1}(\xi)([\gamma])=+1
if the monodromy of
\gamma^{*}\xi
preserves the orientation of the fibres, and
w_{1}(\xi)([\gamma])=-1
otherwise.

Given an
S^{1}
-bundle
\xi\colon M\to B
which admits a section, we classify which relative
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology classes in
M
are represented by the images of sections of
\xi
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
B
be a compact
n
-manifold and let
\xi\colon M\to B
be an
S^{1}
-bundle which admits a section
s\colon B\to M
. Fix
\beta\in H_{n}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.
Then there is a section
s^{\prime}\colon B\to M
of
\xi
with
s_{*}[B]+s^{\prime}_{*}[B]=\beta
if and only if

\beta\in\im\Big(H^{1}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})\xrightarrow{\rho}H^{1}(B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\xi^{*}}H^{1}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\PD}H_{n}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\Big).

Proof.

We explicitly construct a bijection between
\im(\PD\xi^{*}\rho)\subseteq H_{n}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and sections of
\xi
up to
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology. Note that by working over each connected component separately, we may assume that
B
is connected.

Since
\xi
admits a section
s
, there is a bijection

\delta(s,-)\colon\frac{\{s^{\prime}\colon B\to M\text{ a section of }\xi\}}{\text{homotopy}}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})

given by the obstruction-theoretic primary difference
[
32
, Corollary VI.6.16]
. For a section
s^{\prime}\colon B\to M
of
\xi
, write
\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})\coloneq\rho{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})\in H^{1}(B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
for the reduction of the primary difference mod 2.

We describe
\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})
for two sections
s,s^{\prime}
of
\xi
. Identify
H^{1}(B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\cong\Hom(H_{1}(B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
via the map
\alpha\mapsto\left\langle\alpha,-\right\rangle
.
Choose a map
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to B
, and consider the pullback bundle
\gamma^{*}\xi\colon\gamma^{*}M\to S^{1}
with the pullback sections
\gamma^{*}s
and
\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}
of
\gamma^{*}\xi
.
By the naturality of the primary difference,

\gamma^{*}\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})=\overline{\delta}(\gamma^{*}s,\gamma^{*}s^{\prime})\in H^{1}(S^{1};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}.

Geometrically, this reduced primary difference between
\gamma^{*}s
and
\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}
corresponds to the difference in how many times
\gamma^{*}s
and
\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}
twist around the fibre, counted mod 2. Hence it is given by the algebraic intersection number of
\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})
and
\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}(S^{1})
in
\gamma^{*}M
. Explicitly,

(
\dagger
)

\left\langle\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime}),[\gamma(S^{1})]\right\rangle=\left\langle\overline{\delta}(\gamma^{*}s,\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}),[S^{1}]\right\rangle=[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}(S^{1})]\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}.

Since
\gamma^{*}M
has total space either a Klein bottle or a torus, for any other section
u\colon S^{1}\to\gamma^{*}M
of
\gamma^{*}\xi
, we have that

[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}(S^{1})]=[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})]+[\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})],

and hence by (

†

‣
3.4
) that

(
\ddagger
)

\left\langle\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime}),[\gamma(S^{1})]\right\rangle=[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})]+[\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})].

Any class in
H_{1}(B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
is represented by the image of a circle, so this fully describes
\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})
.

Next, we describe
\xi^{*}\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})\in H^{1}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Fix a map
\alpha\colon S^{1}\to M
and write
\gamma\coloneq\xi\alpha\colon S^{1}\to B
. We can assume that
\alpha
and
s
are transverse and intersect away from any double points of
\alpha
.

There is a section
u\colon S^{1}\to\gamma^{*}M
of
\gamma^{*}\xi
given by

u(\theta)\coloneq(\theta,\alpha(\theta))\in\gamma^{*}M=\big\{(t,e)\in S^{1}\times M\mid\gamma(t)=\xi(e)\big\}.

See
??
. This has the property that
[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})]=[s(B)]\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})]\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
, where the first algebraic intersection is taken in
\gamma^{*}M
and the final one is taken in
M
. This follows from the following computation:

M

Figure 4.
A simple model, demonstrating that
[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})]=[s(B)]\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})]
. Left: the

, above

; a curve

) in

(resp.

.

\displaystyle[\gamma^{*}s(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})]

\displaystyle=\#\{\theta\in S^{1}\mid s\gamma(\theta)=\alpha(\theta)\}\bmod{2}

\displaystyle=\#\{(x,\theta)\in B\times S^{1}\mid s(x)=\alpha(\theta)\}\bmod{2}

\displaystyle=[s(B)]\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})]\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}.

The second equality follows because the equation
s(x)=\alpha(\theta)
implies
x=\gamma(\theta)
. Similarly, we get that

[\gamma^{*}s^{\prime}(S^{1})]\cdot[u(S^{1})]=[s^{\prime}(B)]\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})].

Applying (

‡

‣
3.4
) with this choice of
u
gives that

\displaystyle\left\langle\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime}),[\gamma(S^{1})]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=[s(B)]\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})]+[s^{\prime}(B)]\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})]

\displaystyle=\left(s_{*}[B]+s^{\prime}_{*}[B]\right)\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})],

and hence that

\displaystyle\left\langle\xi^{*}\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime}),[\alpha(S^{1})]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime}),[\gamma(S^{1})]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(s_{*}[B]+s^{\prime}_{*}[B]\right)\cdot[\alpha(S^{1})].

Thus
\xi^{*}\overline{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})
is Poincaré dual to
s_{*}[B]+s^{\prime}_{*}[B]\in H_{n-1}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, or equivalently

s_{*}[B]+s^{\prime}_{*}[B]=\PD\xi^{*}\rho\delta(s,s^{\prime}).

Thus the image of the realisation map

\frac{\{s^{\prime}\colon B\to M\text{ a section of }\xi\}}{\text{homotopy}}\to H_{n}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})

given by
[s^{\prime}]\mapsto s_{*}[B]+s^{\prime}_{*}[B]
is exactly the image of the composition

\frac{\{s^{\prime}\colon B\to M\text{ a section of }\xi\}}{\text{homotopy}}\xrightarrow{\delta(s,-)}H^{1}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})\xrightarrow{\PD\xi^{*}\rho}H_{n}(M,\partial M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

The final result follows since
\delta(s,-)
is a bijection.
∎

Since
SN_{X}\Sigma
and
S\nu\Sigma
are isomorphic
S^{1}
-bundles, they both have the orientation character
w_{1}(\pi)\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\{\pm 1\}
. This allows us to classify when every possible codimension 1 relative
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology class of
S\nu\Sigma
is realised by a push-off of
\Sigma
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a section. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

For each
\alpha\in H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, there is a section
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
such that
[\Sigma^{s}_{+}]=\alpha
if and only if
\pi_{*}\alpha=[\Sigma]\in H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

(ii)

The 3-term sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);\mathbb{Z}/2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PD\pi^{*}\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

is exact.

(iii)

The abelian group
H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion.

Proof.

We first show that (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii). Since
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a section, we may fix a section
s\colon\Sigma\to S\nu\Sigma
of
\pi
. Fix a class
\alpha\in H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. By
??
, there is a section
s^{\prime}
of
\pi
with
s^{\prime}_{*}[\Sigma]=\alpha
if and only if
\alpha+s_{*}[\Sigma]\in\im(\PD\pi^{*}\rho)
. But
\pi_{*}\alpha=[\Sigma]
if and only if
\alpha+s_{*}[\Sigma]\in\ker\pi_{*}
. Thus (i) is equivalent to
\im(\PD\pi^{*}\rho)=\ker\pi_{*}
, i.e. that the sequence in (ii) is exact. So (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii).

We now show that (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii). This follows from two claims: first, that
\pi^{*}
is injective and
\im(\PD\pi^{*})=\ker\pi_{*}
; and second, that
\rho
is surjective if and only if
H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion.

For the first claim, consider the Gysin sequence of the fibration
\pi
, which begins

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n-1}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

Here,
\pi_{!}
is the umkehr map defined by the composition

H_{n-1}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\PD^{-1}}H^{1}(\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}}H^{1}(S\nu\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\PD}H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),

which is given explicitly on a relative
(n-1)
-cycle
\sigma
in
(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)
by
\pi_{!}[\sigma]=[\pi^{-1}(\sigma)]
.
Since Poincaré duality maps are isomorphisms and
\pi_{!}
is injective by exactness,
\pi^{*}
must be injective, and

\im(\PD\pi^{*})=\im\pi_{!}=\ker\pi_{*}.

This proves the first claim.

For the second claim, consider the short exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(\Sigma)]
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 2}

This induces the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 2}

where
\beta
is a Bockstein homomorphism. Then by exactness,
\rho
is surjective if and only if
\beta=0
, if and only if
H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion.
∎

Finally we deduce
??
, giving sufficient conditions for
\Sigma
to admit a spanning manifold.

Theorem

(
??
)
.

Let
X
be an
(n+2)
-manifold and let
\Sigma\subset X
be a properly embedded compact
n
-manifold. Let
w_{1}(\pi)\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\Aut(\mathbb{Z})=\{\pm 1\}
be the orientation character of
SN_{X}\Sigma
, and suppose that
H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion. Then
\Sigma
admits a spanning manifold if and only if
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a section and
[\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

Proof.

The forward direction is clear, and holds without the assumption that
H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion. If
\Sigma
admits a spanning manifold, then it must be null-homologous, since it bounds a relative chain in
(X,\partial X)
.
It must also admit a Seifert section, and so
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a section.

We now prove the reverse. By
??
, if
\Sigma
is null-homologous then there is a class

\alpha\in\im\partial_{S}\subseteq H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})

such that
\pi_{*}\alpha=[\Sigma]\in H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.
Since
H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion and
SN_{X}\Sigma
admits a section by assumption,
??
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) says that there is a section
s\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
such that
[\Sigma_{+}^{s}]=\alpha
. Hence
s
is a Seifert section, and by
??
it is the Seifert section associated to some spanning manifold for
\Sigma
.
∎

If
H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has non-trivial 2-torsion, more careful examination of the inclusion
\Sigma\subset X
is required to establish the existence of a spanning manifold. We do not investigate this further.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
s

. The following statements are equivalent.

Corollary
\thetheorem
.

Suppose
H^{2}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion. Let
s
be a section of
{SN_{X}\Sigma}
, and fix a class
\alpha\in H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Then there exists a section
s^{\prime}\colon\Sigma\to SN_{X}\Sigma
extending
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
such that
[\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}]=\alpha
if and only if
\pi_{*}\alpha=[\Sigma]\in H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
\partial\alpha=[\partial\Sigma^{s}_{+}]\in H_{n-1}(\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

Proof.

The forward direction is clear, so we prove the reverse.
Consider the diagram below, which commutes and has exact rows by naturality of the long exact sequence of the pair:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma,\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n-1}(\partial(S\nu\Sigma);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}}

Since
\partial\alpha=\partial[\Sigma^{s}_{+}]
, exactness says that there is some
\alpha^{\prime}\in H_{n}(S\nu\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
such that
i_{*}\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha+[\Sigma^{s}_{+}]
. Then because
\pi_{*}\alpha=[\Sigma]
, we see that
\pi_{*}i_{*}\alpha^{\prime}=j_{*}\pi_{*}\alpha^{\prime}=0
. Finally, since

is injective by exactness,
\pi_{*}\alpha^{\prime}=0
.

By
??
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) and the assumption that
H^{2}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
has no 2-torsion, there is a section
s^{\prime}
of
SN_{X}\Sigma
extending
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
such that

and
