As outlined in
??
, the first step in our construction of cobordisms between surfaces will be to construct spanning 3-manifolds of embedded surfaces coming from puncturing immersed surfaces around their double points.
??
will allow us to do this in such a way that we can guarantee that these spanning manifolds extend annuli on the boundary 3-spheres introduced by puncturing at the double points.

In this case, the 2-torsion condition simplifies, and is automatically satisfied when
X
is orientable. To see this, note that if
\Sigma
is a compact surface, then

H^{2}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})\cong H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(T\Sigma)+w_{1}(\pi)})=H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-2.1097pt]{0.0pt}{7.79723pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-1.50694pt]{0.0pt}{7.19447pt}}_{\Sigma}}{{TX\rule[-2.1097pt]{0.0pt}{7.79723pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-1.50694pt]{0.0pt}{7.19447pt}}_{\Sigma}}{{TX\rule[-2.1097pt]{0.0pt}{6.36221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-1.50694pt]{0.0pt}{5.75946pt}}_{\Sigma}}{{TX\rule[-2.1097pt]{0.0pt}{5.40555pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-1.50694pt]{0.0pt}{4.8028pt}}_{\Sigma}})}).

So if
X
is orientable so that
w_{1}(TX)
is trivial, then
H^{2}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\pi)})
is a free abelian group, so in particular has no 2-torsion. So
??
allows us to completely classify when a spanning surface for the boundary
\partial\Sigma
extends to a spanning manifold for
\Sigma
, without any additional assumptions of the embedding of
\Sigma
into
X
.

4.
Relative normal Euler numbers

In this section, we review the normal Euler number of an immersed submanifold, and prove several important technical lemmas. Much of this is well-known to experts, but is recorded for ease of reference and since proofs are not always easily found in the literature. We continue to only work explicitly in the smooth category, though all proofs can be modified to work in the topological category as well.

In
??
, we recall the basic definitions in terms of counting intersection points. In
??
, we discuss the normal Euler number as a cobordism obstruction. Section
4.3
gives a lemma explaining how to use the mod 2 reduction of the relative normal Euler number to compute the algebraic intersection number between two properly immersed submanifolds with disjoint boundaries. Finally,
??
specialises to the case of surfaces with non-empty boundaries properly immersed in oriented 4-manifolds.

4.1.
Relative normal Euler numbers

We give the definition of the relative Euler number of a disc bundle with oriented total space in terms of counting intersections.

Definition
\thetheorem
.

Let
B
be a compact
n
-manifold, let
\xi\colon M\to B
be a
D^{n}
-bundle with the total space
M
oriented, and let
s
be a section of the sphere bundle
\mathchoice{{S\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial B}}{{S\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial B}}{{S\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{\partial B}}{{S\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{\partial B}}
. Let
s^{\prime}
be any section of
\xi
with
\mathchoice{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{12.32336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{11.46228pt}}_{\partial B}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.82336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.96228pt}}_{\partial B}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{10.10114pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{9.24005pt}}_{\partial B}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.52777pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.66669pt}}_{\partial B}}=s
. Identify
B
with the zero section of
\xi
, and assume that
s^{\prime}
meets
B
transversely. For each point
p\in B\cap s^{\prime}(B)
, choose an arbitrary orientation of the tangent space
T_{p}B
. This induces an orientation of

T_{p}B\oplus s^{\prime}_{*}T_{p}B=T_{p}M.

Let
\varepsilon_{p}=+1
if this orientation agrees with the orientation of
T_{p}M
coming from the orientation of
M
, and let
\varepsilon_{p}=-1
otherwise. The relative Euler number
e(\xi,s)
is the sum

\sum_{p\in B\cap s^{\prime}(B)}\varepsilon_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}.

If
\partial B=\varnothing
, we simply write
e(\xi)
. If
B_{0}
is a union of connected components of
B
, we abuse notation and write
e(\mathchoice{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{B_{0}}},s)
instead of
e(\mathchoice{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{B_{0}}},\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{B_{0}}})
.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

The relative Euler number
e(\xi,s)
also has an algebraic formulation. The primary obstruction to the existence of a section of
S\xi
extending
s
is
\theta(s)\in H^{n}(B,\partial B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})
. Since
M
is orientable,
w_{1}(\xi)+w_{1}(TB)\colon\pi_{1}(B)\to\Aut(\mathbb{Z})
is trivial. Then
e(\xi,s)
is the image of
\theta(s)
under the composition

H^{n}(B,\partial B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})\xrightarrow{\PD}H_{0}(B;\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}^{\pi_{0}(B)}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathbb{Z},

where
\varepsilon(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k}
is the augmentation map.
See Chapters 9 & 12 of
[
24
]
or Section 2 of
[
9
]
for a more detailed treatment of this approach.

Over a connected base space, the relative Euler number is the complete obstruction to the existence of a non-vanishing section of a disc bundle, which extends a given section on the boundary. This follows directly from the algebraic definition, but can also be shown from
??
by cancelling intersection points of opposite sign.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

With notation as in
??
,
s
extends to a section of
S\xi
if and only if
e(\mathchoice{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{B_{0}}}{{\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{B_{0}}},s)=0
for each connected component
B_{0}
of
B
.

Now let
X
be an oriented
2n
-manifold, let
S
be a compact
n
-manifold, and let
f\colon S\looparrowright X
be a proper immersion. Write
\Sigma\coloneq f(S)\subset X
. Recall that any immersion
f
admits a normal bundle
N(f)\to S
which fits in the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}TX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of vector bundles over
S
.
Since
X
is orientable, the orientation characters
w_{1}(TX)
and
w_{1}(f^{*}TX)
are trivial.
Since orientation characters are additive under extension,
w_{1}(N(f))=w_{1}(TS)
, which means that the total space of
N(f)
is orientable. The choice of orientation on
X
also determines one on the total space of
N(f)
via
f
.

If
f
is proper, then
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial S}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial S}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{\partial S}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{\partial S}}
is an embedding, and so there is a bundle isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{{DN(f)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}{{DN(f)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}{{DN(f)\rule[-4.76385pt]{0.0pt}{11.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.90277pt]{0.0pt}{10.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}{{DN(f)\rule[-4.26385pt]{0.0pt}{9.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.40277pt]{0.0pt}{8.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{DN_{\partial X}{\partial\Sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\Sigma.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{f\rule[-3.4708pt]{0.0pt}{9.23608pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.86804pt]{0.0pt}{8.63333pt}}_{\partial S}}{{f\rule[-3.4708pt]{0.0pt}{9.23608pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.86804pt]{0.0pt}{8.63333pt}}_{\partial S}}{{f\rule[-3.06247pt]{0.0pt}{7.36943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.45972pt]{0.0pt}{6.76668pt}}_{\partial S}}{{f\rule[-2.79024pt]{0.0pt}{6.12497pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.42194pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.18748pt]{0.0pt}{5.52222pt}}_{\partial S}}}

Hence for any section
s\colon\partial\Sigma\to SN_{\partial X}{\partial\Sigma}
, we can define the
relative normal Euler number

e(\Sigma,s)\coloneq e\left(DN(f),f_{*}^{-1}\mathchoice{{sf\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial S}}{{sf\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial S}}{{sf\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{\partial S}}{{sf\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{\partial S}}\right).

The value of
e(\Sigma,s)
only depends on the isotopy class of
s
and on the image
\Sigma
, but not the choice of immersion
f
. It depends on the orientation of
X
only up to sign, since reversing the orientation replaces
e(\Sigma,s)
with
-e(\Sigma,s)
. Hence we can make sense of the statement
e(\Sigma,s)=0
without fixing an orientation of
X
. Equally, if
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
are two compact properly immersed submanifolds and
s_{i}\colon\partial\Sigma_{i}\to SN_{\partial X}{\partial\Sigma_{i}}
is a section for
i=0,1
, we can make sense of the statement
e(\Sigma_{0},s_{0})=e(\Sigma_{1},s_{1})
without fixing an orientation of
X
.

4.2.
Relative normal Euler numbers as cobordism invariants

Although it is not in general true that Euler characteristics are an obstruction to (abstract) cobordism, normal Euler numbers do give an obstruction to properly embedded submanifolds being cobordant. Specifically, if
X
is an orientable
2n
-manifold with
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
properly embedded
n
-manifolds, and there is a cobordism
Y\subset X\times I
extending
Z=Y\cap(\partial X\times I)
, then the normal Euler number
e(\Sigma_{0}\mathord{\times}\{0\}\cup Z\cup\Sigma_{1}\mathord{\times}\{1\})
must vanish. This was observed in the case that
X=\mathbb{R}^{4}
in
[
6
]
, but in general follows from the lemma below.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
M
be an orientable
(2n+1)
-manifold and let
Y\subset M
be a properly embedded compact
(n+1)
-manifold. Then
e(\partial Y)=0
, where
\partial Y
is viewed a closed
n
-manifold embedded in the orientable
2n
-manifold
\partial M
.

Proof.

Fix any orientation on
M
.
Let
(\nu Y,\varphi)
be a tubular neighbourhood of
Y
and let
s\colon Y\to DN_{M}Y
be a section that meets the zero-section transversely. Write
Y^{s}_{+}\coloneqq\varphi s(Y)
for the push-off of
Y
in the direction of
s
. Then we may assume that
Y\cap Y^{s}_{+}
consists of a collection of circles and arcs properly embedded in
Y
.

We show
e(\partial Y)=0
using
\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial Y}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial Y}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial Y}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial Y}}
as in
??
. Since all points of intersection in
\partial Y\cap\partial Y^{s}_{+}
are endpoints of some properly embedded arc
\gamma\subseteq Y\cap Y^{s}_{+}
, it suffices to show that the two endpoints of each such arc contribute opposite signs to the sum in
??
.

Choose an arc
\gamma\subseteq Y\cap Y^{s}_{+}
with endpoints
p,q\in\partial Y
. Orient
\gamma
from
p
to
q
. Fix some orientation on
T_{p}\partial Y
, and give
T_{p}\partial Y^{s}_{+}=(\varphi s)_{*}T_{p}Y
the induced orientation. Without loss of generality, assume that the induced orientation on

T_{p}M=T_{p}\gamma\oplus T_{p}\partial Y\oplus T_{p}\partial Y^{s}_{+}

agrees with the orientation coming from the fixed orientation on
\partial M
, else consider the opposite orientation on
M
. Since
T_{p}\gamma
is oriented in the direction of the inwards normal in
\mathchoice{{N_{M}\partial M\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.74998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.8889pt}}_{p}}{{N_{M}\partial M\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.74998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.8889pt}}_{p}}{{N_{M}\partial M\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.1722pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.31111pt}}_{p}}{{N_{M}\partial M\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.4722pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.61111pt}}_{p}}
, the induced orientation of
T_{p}\partial M
disagrees with the orientation coming from
T_{p}\partial Y\oplus T_{p}\partial Y^{s}_{+}
. In the notation of
??
, this means
\varepsilon_{p}=-1
.

However, parallel transporting the orientation of
T_{p}\partial Y
along
\gamma
to
q
, we see that the orientation on
T_{q}M
also agrees with the orientation coming from
T_{q}\gamma\oplus T_{q}\partial Y\oplus T_{q}\partial Y^{s}_{+}
. However, since
T_{q}\gamma
is oriented in the direction of the outward normal, the induced orientation of
T_{q}\partial M
agrees with the orientation coming from
T_{q}\partial Y\oplus T_{q}\partial Y^{s}_{+}
, so
\varepsilon_{q}=+1
.

Thus the two endpoints of
\gamma
contribute opposite signs, and we are done.
∎

4.3.
Computing intersections between immersed submanifolds

Let
X
be an oriented
2n
-manifold, let
S
be a compact
n
-manifold, and let
f\colon S\looparrowright X
be a proper immersion. Write
\Sigma\coloneq f(S)\subset X
.
If
\partial S=\varnothing
, it is a standard result that
e(\Sigma)\bmod{2}
agrees with the algebraic intersection
[\Sigma]\cdot[\Sigma]\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
. This is because both
e(\Sigma)\bmod{2}
and
[\Sigma]\cdot[\Sigma]
are given by the mod 2 count of intersections between
\Sigma
and a push-off (or perturbation) of
\Sigma
which is chosen to intersect
\Sigma
transversely.

The same argument works relative to a section
s\colon\partial\Sigma\to SN_{\partial X}\partial\Sigma
on the boundary, although one must take the algebraic intersection of
\Sigma
with a push-off in the direction of a section which extends
s
. The details of this appear to be missing from the literature, save a special case which appears as Lemma 13.8 in
[
8
]
. In this subsection we give these details, and deduce a result that relates relative normal Euler numbers to the algebraic intersection of two proper immersions with disjoint boundaries.

First, recall that whenever
A,B\subseteq\partial X
are closed and have disjoint deformation retracts, there is an algebraic intersection form

-\cdot-\colon H_{n}(X,A;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\times H_{n}(X,B;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\to{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}.

This is realised by counting the intersections mod 2 of representing properly embedded
n
-manifolds which intersect transversely. In particular, it makes sense to discuss the algebraic intersection number of two properly immersed
n
-manifolds with disjoint boundaries, by taking
A
to be a neighbourhood of the boundary of one, and taking
B\coloneq\overline{\partial X\setminus A}
.

We now discuss how to formalise a push-off of the immersion
f
. Write
\pi\colon DN(f)\to S
for the projection map. There exists an immersion
\vartheta\colon DN(f)\looparrowright X
which restricts to
f\colon S\looparrowright X
on the zero-section and is such that every point
x\in S
has a neighbourhood
U\subseteq S
such that
\mathchoice{{\vartheta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.24998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.3889pt}}_{\pi^{-1}(U)}}{{\vartheta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.24998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.3889pt}}_{\pi^{-1}(U)}}{{\vartheta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.16664pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.30556pt}}_{\pi^{-1}(U)}}{{\vartheta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.77776pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.91667pt}}_{\pi^{-1}(U)}}\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to X
is an embedding. We may also assume that
\nu\partial\Sigma\coloneq\vartheta(\pi^{-1}(\partial S))
is a tubular neighbourhood for
\partial\Sigma
in
\partial X
.
Then for any section
s^{\prime}\colon S\to DN(f)
of
\pi
which is transverse to the zero section, write
\Sigma_{+}^{s^{\prime}}\coloneq\vartheta s^{\prime}(S)\subset X
. This can be seen as a push-off of
\Sigma
in the direction of
s^{\prime}
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Fix a section
s\colon\partial\Sigma\to SN_{\partial X}\partial\Sigma
, and any section
s^{\prime}\colon S\to DN(f)
with

\mathchoice{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{12.32336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{11.46228pt}}_{\partial S}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.82336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.96228pt}}_{\partial S}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{10.10114pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{9.24005pt}}_{\partial S}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.52777pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.66669pt}}_{\partial S}}=f_{*}^{-1}\mathchoice{{sf\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial S}}{{sf\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{\partial S}}{{sf\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{\partial S}}{{sf\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{\partial S}}\colon\partial S\to\mathchoice{{SN(f)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}{{SN(f)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}{{SN(f)\rule[-4.76385pt]{0.0pt}{11.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.90277pt]{0.0pt}{10.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}{{SN(f)\rule[-4.26385pt]{0.0pt}{9.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.40277pt]{0.0pt}{8.44446pt}}_{\partial S}}.

Then
[\Sigma]\cdot[\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}]=e(\Sigma,s)\bmod 2
.

Proof.

We may assume that
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}
intersect in isolated transverse double points, so this algebraic intersection number is just the count of intersections between
\Sigma
and
\Sigma_{+}^{s^{\prime}}
mod 2. By definition, exactly
e(\Sigma,s)
such intersections occur away from the double points of
f
, counted with sign. Each double point of
f
gives two more intersections between
\Sigma
and
\Sigma_{+}^{s^{\prime}}
, given by each sheet of
\Sigma
intersecting the opposite sheet of
\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}
. But these do not contribute to the count mod 2.
∎

??
allows us compare the relative Euler numbers of two homologous properly immersed submanifolds.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
S=S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}
is a disjoint union of two compact
n
-manifolds, and write
\Sigma_{i}=f(S_{i})
for
i=0,1
so that
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
. Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning manifold for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
, and suppose that
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Then

[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma_{1}]=e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})\bmod 2=e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\bmod 2.

Proof.

Consider the tubular neighbourhood of
\partial\Sigma
given by
\nu\partial\Sigma\coloneq\vartheta\big(\pi^{-1}(\partial S)\big)\subset\partial X,

and let
E_{\partial}\coloneq\overline{\partial X\setminus\nu\partial\Sigma}\subset\partial X
be the exterior of
\partial\Sigma
. Also let

M\coloneq\Sigma\cup(Z\cap\nu\partial\Sigma)\subset X

be the subspace given by extending
\Sigma
along its boundary by a collar of
Z
. Note that
M
is the image of an immersion
S\looparrowright X
, homotopic to
f
, which is not a proper immersion. Finally, let
s^{\prime}\colon S\to DN(f)
be any section extending
s^{Z}
, and let
\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}\coloneq\vartheta s^{\prime}(S)
be the push-off of
\Sigma
in the direction of
s^{\prime}
. Then
\partial M=\partial\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}
, and in fact
\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}
and
M
are homotopic rel.
\partial\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}
. So in
H_{n}(X,E_{\partial};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
,

\displaystyle[\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}]=[M]=[\Sigma\cup(Z\cap\nu\partial\Sigma)]

\displaystyle=[\Sigma\cup(Z\setminus E_{\partial})]

\displaystyle=[Z\cup\Sigma]-[Z\cap E_{\partial}]

\displaystyle=0\in H_{n}(X,E_{\partial};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

Here the last line follows since
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
by assumption, and
Z\cap E_{\partial}\subset E_{\partial}
.

Finally, let
f^{\prime}\colon S\looparrowright X
be some immersion, homotopic to
f
, with image
\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}
. Write
\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}\coloneq f^{\prime}(S_{i})
for
i=0,1
, and note that
[\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}]=[\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}]\in H_{n}(X,E_{\partial};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
since
[\Sigma^{s^{\prime}}_{+}]=[\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}]=0
by the previous paragraph. Hence

[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma_{1}]=[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}]=[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}]\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}},

where the first equality follows
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
is homotopic to
\Sigma_{1}
rel. the exterior of
\partial\Sigma_{0}
.
Finally,
??
gives that

[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}]=e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})\bmod{2},

and the result follows.
∎

Remark
\thetheorem
.

By taking
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}=\varnothing
, repeating this argument with
\mathbb{Z}
-homology gives a proof that if
\Sigma
is closed, oriented, embedded, and
[\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, then
e(\Sigma)=0
. If
\Sigma
is closed and non-orientable,
e(\Sigma)
can be non-zero even if
\Sigma
is embedded and null-homologous. For example, results of Whitney and Massey show that if
\Sigma\subset S^{4}
is an embedded closed connected non-orientable surface of Euler characteristic
\chi
, then

e(\Sigma)\in\{2\chi-4,2\chi,2\chi+4,\ldots,4-2\chi\},

and that all these normal Euler numbers are achieved
[
33
]
,
[
23
]
.

4.4.
Knot framings and relative normal Euler numbers of surfaces

Before discussing surfaces properly immersed in oriented 4-manifolds, we recall some facts about framings of 1-manifolds in oriented 3-manifolds.

Let
Y
be an oriented 3-manifold. Let
L\subset Y
be an embedded closed 1-manifold (i.e. a link in
Y
) and let
(\nu L,\varphi)
be a tubular neighbourhood. Then the normal bundle
N_{Y}L
is trivial, and after fixing an orientation of
L
, two sections
s,s^{\prime}\colon L\to SN_{Y}L
are isotopic if and only if
s_{*}[L]=s^{\prime}_{*}[L]\in H_{1}(SN_{Y}L;\mathbb{Z})
. In this case, we refer to an isotopy class of sections of
SN_{Y}L
as a
framing
of
L
. A
Seifert framing
of
L
is a framing that contains Seifert sections. We regularly abuse notation by using the same symbol to refer to a section of
SN_{Y}L
and the framing it represents.

Let
K\subset L
be a connected component of
L
. If
Y=S^{3}
with the usual orientation, there is a canonical identification

\fr_{K}\colon\{\text{framings of }K\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}

given by the linking number,

\fr_{K}(s)\coloneq\lk(K,s(K)).

For general oriented
Y
, the set of framings of
K
still has a
\mathbb{Z}
-torsor structure, which we denote by
\star
. Let
\nu K
be the component of
\nu L
containing
K
. The orientation on
Y
induces one on
\nu K
and
S\nu K
, and hence one on
SN_{Y}K
via the identification
\varphi\colon DN_{Y}L\to\nu L
. Choose an orientation on
K
, which in turn induces one on the
S^{1}
-fibres of
SN_{Y}K
. Then for a fixed framing
s
and integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a unique framing
s^{\prime}
of
K
such that the algebraic intersection number
s_{*}[K]\cdot s^{\prime}_{*}[K]=n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This framing is independent of the choice of orientation of
K
, so we write
n\star s=s^{\prime}
. Geometrically,
n
acts by adding
n
twists in the positive direction.
If
s
is a framing of
L
, we abuse notation and write
\fr_{K}(s)
instead of
\fr_{K}(\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{K}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{K}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{K}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{K}})
.

Now return to the situation where
X
is an oriented 4-manifold,
S
a compact surface, and
f\colon S\looparrowright X
a proper immersion with image
\Sigma\coloneq f(S)
. Then the
\mathbb{Z}
-action on the framings of
\partial\Sigma\subset\partial X
intertwines the relative normal Euler number of
\Sigma
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Fix a component
K
of
\partial\Sigma
. Let
s,s^{\prime}
be framings of
\partial\Sigma
which agree on
\partial\Sigma\setminus K
. If
\mathchoice{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{12.32336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{11.46228pt}}_{K}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.82336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.96228pt}}_{K}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{10.10114pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{9.24005pt}}_{K}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.52777pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.66669pt}}_{K}}=n\star\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{K}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{K}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{K}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{K}}
, then
e(\Sigma,s^{\prime})=n+e(\Sigma,s)
.

This can be seen from the boundary twisting operation described in Section 1.3 of
[
10
]
; we give a slightly different proof which is similar in spirit.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case that
e(\Sigma,s)=0
. Then we may compute
e(\Sigma,s^{\prime})
using an extension of
s^{\prime}
to
S
which is nowhere-vanishing outside of a collar of
K
, and which is the trace of an isotopy from
s^{\prime}
to
s
in this collar. Then
e(\Sigma,s^{\prime})
is the signed count of intersections of this isotopy with the zero-section in the collar. Thus it suffices to replace
\Sigma
with just this collar of
K
, that is to consider the case that
X=S^{1}\times I\times D^{2}
,
\Sigma=S^{1}\times I\times\{0\}
.
By arranging the intersection points to occur in different levels
S^{1}\times\{\pt\}\subset\Sigma
, it also suffices to consider that case
n=1
.

Finally, by viewing
S^{1}\times I
as a collar neighbourhood of the boundary of
D^{2}
, it in fact suffices to consider the case
X=D^{2}\times D^{2}
,
\Sigma=D^{2}\times\{0\}
,
n=1
, and
e(\Sigma,s)=0
. In this case, explicit representatives
s,s^{\prime}\colon S^{1}\times\{0\}\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
can be given by
s(x,0)=(x,\{1\})
and
s^{\prime}(x,0)=(x,x)
. Then
s^{\prime}=1\star s
. By considering the extensions
D^{2}\times\{0\}\to D^{2}\times D^{2}
given by the same formulae, we see that
e(\Sigma,s)=0
and
e(\Sigma,s^{\prime})=1
.
∎

We can also rewrite
1.3
as follows in the case
n=2
.

Corollary
\thetheorem
.

Let
X
be an orientable 4-manifold and let
\Sigma\subset X
be a properly embedded compact surface.
Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma
and let
s^{Z}
be the associated Seifert section.
Then
\Sigma
admits a spanning manifold extending
Z
if and only if
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{n}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})=0
for each connected component
\Sigma_{0}\subseteq\Sigma
.

This follows from
??
and recalling that the 2-torsion condition is automatically satisfied when
X
is orientable.

5.
Spanning manifolds of punctured immersed surfaces

In order to construct cobordisms between properly embedded surfaces in 4-manifolds from spanning 3-manifolds, we need to develop a theory of spanning 3-manifolds for pairs of properly embedded surfaces, which may intersect. Their union is then an immersed surface, which is considered up to isotopy of the two surfaces.

To this end, we develop a theory of spanning 3-manifolds for arbitrary properly immersed surfaces in oriented 4-manifolds. To do this, we puncture the ambient 4-manifold around the double points of the immersion to yield a proper embedding, and then only consider spanning 3-manifolds which extend annuli on the
S^{3}
boundary components introduced by puncturing. We give conditions for such spanning 3-manifolds exist and have prescribed boundary when the immersion is allowed to be modified either by any regular homotopy, or only by a regular homotopy which decomposes as a pair of isotopies, at least one of which is constant on each connected component of the surface.

We now make this precise. Let
X
be an oriented 4-manifold, let
S
be a compact surface, let
f\colon S\looparrowright X
be a proper immersion, and write
\Sigma\coloneq f(S)
.
Since we assume
f
is generic, all self-intersections of
\Sigma
are transverse double points. Each such double point
p
has a 4-ball neighbourhood
D\subset X
such that the pair
(D,D\cap\Sigma)
is diffeomorphic to
(D^{4},D^{2}\mathord{\times}\{0,0\}\cup\{0,0\}\mathord{\times}D^{2})
. The pair
(\partial D,\partial D\cap\Sigma)
a Hopf link in
S^{3}

[
13
, §4.6]
. We call the immersion

\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{f^{-1}(\overline{X\setminus D})}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{f^{-1}(\overline{X\setminus D})}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{f^{-1}(\overline{X\setminus D})}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{f^{-1}(\overline{X\setminus D})}}\colon f^{-1}(\overline{X\setminus D})\looparrowright\overline{X\setminus D}

the
immersion of
f
punctured at
p
.

Definition
\thetheorem
.

The punctured embedding of
f
is the embedding
\widehat{f}\colon\widehat{S}\to\widehat{X}
given by puncturing
f
at all double points, so that
\widehat{\Sigma}\coloneq\widehat{f}(\widehat{S})
is properly embedded in
\widehat{X}
. If
Z\subset\partial X
is a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma
, we say that
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma
if there is a spanning manifold
\widehat{Y}
for
\widehat{\Sigma}
such that
\widehat{Y}\cap\partial X=Z
, and for each component
\partial_{0}\widehat{X}\subseteq\partial\widehat{X}\setminus\partial X
, the surface
\widehat{Y}\cap\partial_{0}\widehat{X}
is an annulus.

That is,
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma
if we can find annuli spanning the Hopf links in
\partial\widehat{\Sigma}\subset\partial\widehat{X}
introduced by puncturing, such that the union of
Z
with these annuli extends to a spanning 3-manifold of
\widehat{\Sigma}
. Our main aim in this section is prove the following result, which will be an immediate corollary of our more general results.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
X
be an orientable 4-manifold, and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be two properly embedded compact surfaces. Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma_{0}\cup\partial\Sigma_{1}
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
. Then there are surfaces
\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
isotopic to
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
respectively such that
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
if and only if
[\Sigma_{0}\cup Z\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})=e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})
.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

The notion of an almost-extendable spanning surface is inspired by the arguments in
[
1
]
to show that surfaces
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}
in properly embedded in an orientable 4-manifold
X
are weakly internally stably isotopic (that is, can be made isotopic after finitely many ambient self-connected sums) if and only if (i) they are orientable and
\mathbb{Z}
-homologous with some choice of orientations, or (ii) they are non-orientable,
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homologous, and have the same normal Euler number.

We summarise that argument here. After an isotopy, we may assume that each component of
\Sigma_{0}
intersects each component of
\Sigma_{1}
in at least one point. We can then resolve intersections to obtain a properly embedded connected surface
\Sigma\subset X
, by replacing small neighbourhoods of the double points of
\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
by annuli. Under the stated assumptions,
??
says that
\Sigma
admits a spanning manifold
Y\subset X
. Choose a handle decomposition of
Y
rel.
\Sigma_{0}\cap Y
. The 1-handles specify internal stabilisations of
\Sigma_{0}
and the 2-handles specify internal stabilisations of
\Sigma_{1}
, which result in isotopic surfaces. In fact, the two resulting surfaces are equal away from the intersection points, and near an intersection point they are related by an isotopy along the chosen annulus.

This method does not quite suffice for our purposes. In particular, it is important for us that the spanning 3-manifold
Y
lies entirely outside of the neighbourhood of the double points of
\Sigma_{0}\cap\Sigma_{1}
whose boundary contains the annulus resolving the intersection. We ensure this by considering spanning manifolds of the punctured embedding of
\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
, rather than simply resolving intersections.

5.1.
Regular and generic homotopies

A regular homotopy of
f
is a homotopy rel.
\partial S
through immersions. A generic homotopy rel.
\partial S
is a composition of regular homotopies and cusp homotopies. A regular homotopy is generically a composition of isotopies, finger moves, and Whitney moves
[
34
,
35
]
,
[
12
, §III.3]
.
We omit precise definitions of these moves, and instead refer the reader to Chapter 1 of
[
10
]
or Chapter XII of
[
19
]
.

We write
\self(\Sigma)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for the number of self-intersections of
f
, not counted with sign since
S
is unoriented. Note that
\self(\Sigma)
depends only on the image
\Sigma
, justifying the notation.
If
\Sigma
is obtained from
\Sigma^{\prime}
by a finger move or from
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
by a cusp homotopy, then

\self(\Sigma)=\self(\Sigma^{\prime})+2=\self(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})+1,

and

e(\Sigma,s)=e(\Sigma^{\prime},s)=e(\Sigma^{\prime\prime},s)\pm 2

for any framing
s
of
\partial\Sigma
. In particular, neither
e(\Sigma,s)
or
2\self(\Sigma)\bmod{4}
are affected by regular homotopies; a cusp homotopy changes both
e(\Sigma,s)\bmod{4}
and
2\self(\Sigma)\bmod{4}
by
2\in{\mathbb{Z}/{4}}
. In particular, the quantity

e(\Sigma,s)-2\self(\Sigma)\mod{4}

is an invariant of the pair
(\Sigma,s)
under homotopy rel.
\partial\Sigma
.

5.2.
Conditions for a spanning surface to be almost-extendable

In order to give conditions for a spanning surface
Z\subset\partial X
for
\partial\Sigma
to be almost-extendable over
\Sigma
, we need to consider the hypotheses of
??
as applied to the punctured embedding
\widehat{\Sigma}\subset\widehat{X}
. To this end, we check the effect of puncturing on homology and relative normal Euler numbers.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
f^{\prime}\colon S^{\prime}\looparrowright X^{\prime}
be obtained by puncturing
f
at a double point. Write
\Sigma^{\prime}\coloneq f^{\prime}(S^{\prime})
.

(i)

The class
[\Sigma^{\prime}]=0\in H_{2}(X^{\prime},\partial X^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
if and only if
[\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

(ii)

Let
Z\subset\partial X^{\prime}
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then
Z\cap\partial X
is a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma
, and
[Z\cup\Sigma^{\prime}]=0\in H_{2}(X^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
if and only if
[(Z\cap\partial X)\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
.

(iii)

Let
s
be a framing of
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}
and let
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}=\partial\Sigma\sqcup K_{0}\sqcup K_{1}
. Then

e(\Sigma^{\prime},s)=e(\Sigma,s)+\fr_{K_{0}}(s)+\fr_{K_{1}}(s).

Proof.

We prove (i)–(iii) in order. Let
D\subset X
be the 4-ball such that
X^{\prime}=\overline{X\setminus D}
. Then the image of
[\Sigma]
under the composite isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}(X,\partial X\cup D;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}(X^{\prime},\partial X^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is exactly
[\Sigma^{\prime}]
, proving (i).

For (ii), note that the map
H_{2}(X^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\to H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
induced by inclusion is an isomorphism, so it suffices to show that
[Z\cup\Sigma^{\prime}]=[(Z\cap\partial X)\cup\Sigma]\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. This is true, since

[Z\cup\Sigma^{\prime}]+[(Z\cap\partial X)\cup\Sigma]=[(Z\cap D)\cup(\Sigma\cap D)]\in\im(H_{2}(D;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\to H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})),

so must be trivial.

For (iii), let
s^{\prime}
be the framing of
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
\mathchoice{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{12.32336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{11.46228pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.82336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.96228pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{10.10114pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{9.24005pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.52777pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.66669pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}=\mathchoice{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.75pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.31943pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.45834pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{s\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{5.59723pt}}_{\partial\Sigma}}
, but
\fr_{K_{0}}(s^{\prime})=\fr_{K_{1}}(s^{\prime})=0
. By
??
, it suffices to show that
e(\Sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime})=e(\Sigma,s)
. But this is true, since
e(\Sigma,s)
can be computed using the intersections with the zero-section of an extension of
s
which is non-vanishing on
\Sigma\cap D
.
∎

We can now give a combinatorial condition for when a given spanning surface
Z
for
\partial\Sigma
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma
, depending on the specifics of the intersections between different components of
S
under
f
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert framing
s^{Z}
. Let
n=\self(\Sigma)
, and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\Sigma
be the double points of
f
. For each component
C\subset S
and each
i=1,\ldots,n
, let

\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\coloneq\#\big(C\cap f^{-1}(\{p_{i}\})\big)\in\{0,1,2\}

be the number of preimages of
p_{i}
in
C
.
Then
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma
if and only if
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and there exists a choice of
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that for each component
C\subseteq S
,

e(f(C),s^{Z})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}.

Proof.

We will prove the equivalence directly by considering the hypotheses of
??
.
Let
\widehat{f}\colon\widehat{S}\to\widehat{X}
be the punctured embedding of
f
, and write
\widehat{\Sigma}\coloneq\widehat{f}(\widehat{S})
. For each
i=1,\ldots,n
, let
D_{i}\subset X
be the 4-ball neighbourhood of the double point
p_{i}
such that
\widehat{X}=\overline{X\setminus(D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{n})}
.

By
??
,
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma
if and only if, for each
i=1,\ldots,n
, there is an annulus
A_{i}
spanning the Hopf link
\Sigma\cap\partial D_{i}
with associated Seifert section
\widehat{s}_{i}
such that the following two conditions are met.

•

The first condition is that

\Big[Z\cup\bigcup_{i}A_{i}\cup\widehat{\Sigma}\Big]=0\in H_{2}(\widehat{X};{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),

which is equivalent to
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
by
??
(ii).

•

The second condition is that the section
s^{Z}\cup\widehat{s}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widehat{s}_{n}
of
SN_{\partial\widehat{X}}\partial\widehat{\Sigma}
extends to a section of
SN_{\widehat{X}}\widehat{\Sigma}
, or equivalently that

e\big(\widehat{f}(C\cap\widehat{S}),s^{Z}\cup\widehat{s}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widehat{s}_{n}\big)=0.

By
??
(iii), this holds if and only if for each component
C\subseteq S
,

e(f(C),s^{Z})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{K\subseteq f(C)\cap\partial D_{i}}\fr_{K}(\widehat{s}_{i})=0,

where the second sum is taken over the (possibly empty) set of components of
f(C)\cap\partial D_{i}
.

For each double point
p_{i}
, there are two choices of spanning annulus
A_{i}
up to isotopy. If the Hopf link
f(S)\cap\partial D_{i}
has components
K\sqcup K^{\prime}
, then these annuli have Seifert framings specified by

\fr_{K}(\widehat{s}_{i})=\fr_{K^{\prime}}(\widehat{s}_{i})\in\{\pm 1\}.

Hence there exist valid choices of
A_{i}
if and only if we can choose some
\varepsilon_{i}=-\fr_{K}(\widehat{s}_{i})\in\{\pm 1\}
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
such that

\displaystyle e(f(C),s^{Z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{K\subseteq f(C)\cap\partial D_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}.

This completes the proof of equivalence.
∎

5.3.
Conditions for a spanning surface to be almost-extendable after homotopy

The second condition of
??
is very difficult to check in practice.
However, if
f
is allowed to be modified by a regular homotopy, the situation simplifies.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

There exists an immersion
g\colon S\looparrowright X
homotopic rel.
\partial S
to
f
such that
Z
is almost-extendable over
g(S)
.

(ii)

The class
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
e(\Sigma,s^{Z})\equiv 2\self(\Sigma)\bmod{4}
.

In this case,
g
can be taken to be regularly homotopic to
f
. If
S=S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}
is a disjoint union of three non-empty compact surfaces, then the homotopy may be taken to be a sequence of finger moves between
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
, between
S_{0}
and
S_{2}
, and between
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.

Proof.

We first show that (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Suppose we have chosen some immersion
g\colon S\looparrowright X
homotopic to
f
rel.
\partial S
such that
Z
is almost-extendable over
g(S)
. Then
g
satisfies the conditions of
??
; that is,
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, and there exists a choice of
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that for each component
C\subseteq S
,

(
*
)

e(g(C),s^{Z})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}.

Summing over all components
C
in (

∗

‣
5.3
), we see that
e(g(S),s^{Z})=\sum_{i}2\varepsilon_{i}
and hence that

e(g(S),s^{Z})\equiv 2\self(g(S))\mod{4}.

As remarked at the end of
??
, the quantity
e(g(S),s^{Z})-2\self(g(S))\bmod{4}
is an invariant of homotopy rel. boundary, and so

e(\Sigma,s^{Z})-2\self(\Sigma)\equiv e(g(S),s^{Z})-2\self(g(S))\equiv 0\mod{4}.

So (ii) follows as required.

We now show that (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). It suffices to show that the assumption
e(\Sigma,s^{Z})\equiv 2\self(\Sigma)\bmod{4}
guarantees that we can find a suitable immersion
g\colon S\looparrowright X
and signs
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
as in
??
.

Choose some collections of components
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq S
, all non-empty, such that
S=S_{0}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}
. We do not in general assume that the collections are pairwise disjoint; e.g. if
S
is connected, then the choice
S=S_{0}=S_{1}=S_{2}
is forced. We show that there exist finger moves taking
f
to some
g\colon S\looparrowright X
such that suitable
\varepsilon_{i}
can be chosen. Moreover, all finger moves will be constructed between components such that there exist
i\neq j
where the first component is in
S_{i}
and the second is in
S_{j}
. This proves the final remark in the case that
S_{0}
,
S_{1}
, and
S_{2}
are pairwise disjoint.

Suppose some
g\colon S\looparrowright X
which is regularly homotopic to
f
via a suitable sequence of finger moves has been chosen. Let
n\coloneq\self(g(S))
, and let
g
have double points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
. As before, for a component
C\subseteq S
and index
i=1,\ldots,n
, we write
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\coloneq\#\big(C\cap g^{-1}(\{p_{i}\})\big)
. Write also
\mathcal{P}^{C}\coloneq\mathcal{P}^{C}_{1}+\cdots+\mathcal{P}^{C}_{n}
for the total number of preimages of double points in
C
.

By performing more finger moves if necessary, we may assume that
\mathcal{P}^{C}\geq\left\lvert e(g(C),s^{Z})\right\rvert
for each component
C\subseteq S
.
This is because performing finger moves does not affect relative normal Euler numbers, but performing a finger move between
C
and another component increases
\mathcal{P}^{C}
by 2; performing a finger move between
C
and itself increases
\mathcal{P}^{C}
by 4.

We now show that we assume that
\mathcal{P}^{C}\equiv e(g(C),s^{Z})\bmod{4}
for all components
C\subseteq S
.
By
??
and the fact that
[Z\cup g(S)]=[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, we see that for each component
C\subseteq S
,

\mathcal{P}^{C}\bmod{2}=[g(C)]\cdot[g(S\setminus C)]=e(g(C),s^{Z})\bmod{2}.

Since
g
differs from
f
by regular homotopy,

\sum_{C}e(g(C),s^{Z})\equiv e(g(S),s^{Z})\equiv e(\Sigma,s^{Z})\equiv 2\self(\Sigma)\mod{4},

where the final congruence follows by assumption. Moreover, since
g
only differs from
f
by finger moves,

\sum_{C}\mathcal{P}^{C}\equiv 2\self(g(S))\equiv 2\self(\Sigma)\mod{4},

and so
\sum_{C}e(g(C),s^{Z})\equiv\sum_{C}\mathcal{P}^{C}\bmod{4}
.

Suppose there is a component
C\subseteq S
such that
\mathcal{P}^{C}\not\equiv e(g(C),s^{Z})\bmod{4}
. Then there must a second component
C^{\prime}\subseteq S
with
\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}\not\equiv e(g(C^{\prime}),s^{Z})\bmod{4}
. If there are
i\neq j\in\{0,1,2\}
such that
C\subseteq S_{i}
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq S_{j}
, we perform a finger move between
C
and
C^{\prime}
. This does not affect
e(g(C),s^{Z})
or
e(g(C^{\prime}),s^{Z})
, but increases both
\mathcal{P}^{C}
and
\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}
by 2.
If not, suppose
C,C^{\prime}\subset S_{i}
, and choose
j\neq i
and a component
D\subseteq S_{j}
. Then perform a finger move between both
C
and
D
, and
C^{\prime}
and
D
. This again does not affect any relative normal Euler numbers, but increases
\mathcal{P}^{C}
and
\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}
by 2, and
\mathcal{P}^{D}
by 4.
In this way, we can assume that
\mathcal{P}^{C}\equiv e(g(C),s^{Z})\bmod{4}
for all components
C\subseteq S
.

We next describe how to assign a unit
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
to all components
C\subseteq S
and all
i=1,\ldots,n
, such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i}=e(g(C),s^{Z}).

If we can later arrange that the value of
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}
is independent of the component
C\subseteq S
, then we can apply
??
to show that
Z
is almost-extendable over
g
, proving (i).

Fix some component
C\subseteq S
, and let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that

\mathcal{P}^{C}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=e(g(C),s^{Z})+4k.

Since
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\in\{0,1,2\}
for each
i
and
\mathcal{P}^{C}\geq\left\lvert e(g(C),s^{Z})\right\rvert
for each component
C\subseteq S
, we can find a subset
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=2k
. Then

\sum_{i\notin\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}-\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=e(g(C),s^{Z}).

Thus, we let
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}=-1
if
i\in\mathcal{I}
and let
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}=+1
if
i\notin\mathcal{I}
, to get that
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i}=e(g(C),s^{Z})
.

We now wish to perform a sequence of moves on the immersion
g
and the choices of signs
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
, which do not affect
e(g(C),s^{Z})
or the sum
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
for any component
C
, but arrange that for any two components
C,C^{\prime}\subseteq S
and any
i=1,\ldots,n
, we have that
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}=\varepsilon_{i}^{C^{\prime}}
. To do this, we allow three types of moves.

The first move is to find components
C
and
D
, possibly equal, and perform a finger move between them. This introduces two new double points
p_{n+1}
and
p_{n+2}
. We set
\varepsilon^{F}_{n+1}=+1
and
\varepsilon^{F}_{n+2}=-1
for all components
F\subset S
. Performing finger moves does not affect relative normal Euler numbers, and the choice of signs ensure that
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
and
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{D}_{i}\varepsilon^{D}_{i}
are unchanged. Note that in order to perform this move, there must be
i\neq j
such that
C\subseteq S_{i}
and
D\subseteq S_{j}
.

The second move is to find two double points
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
and a component
C
such that
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}=-\varepsilon_{j}^{C}
and
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=\mathcal{P}^{C}_{j}
. We can then swap the signs of
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
and
\varepsilon^{C}_{j}
. This clearly does not affect either
e(g(C),s^{Z})
or the sum
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
for any component
C
.

The third and final move is to find a double point
p_{i}
and a component
C
such that
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=0
. We can then replace
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
with
-\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
. This again affects neither
e(g(C),s^{Z})
nor the sum
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
.

To keep track of the effects of these moves, we assign each double point
p_{i}
(or more precisely, each index
i=1,\ldots,n
) to one of four types (I)–(IV) based on their interactions with the components of
S
. These types are described below.

(I)

For any two components
C,C^{\prime}\subseteq S
,
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}=\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
.

(II)

There exist two components
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
, but in all such cases
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}_{i}=0
.

(III)

There exist two components
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}_{i}=1
, but in all such cases there exist
k\neq\ell\in\{0,1,2\}
such that
C\subseteq S_{k}
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq S_{\ell}
.

(IV)

There exist two components
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}_{i}=1
, and for any
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, either
C,C^{\prime}\subset S_{k}
or
C,C^{\prime}\not\subset S_{k}
.

These should be thought of as follows. If
p_{i}
is type (I), then the signs
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}
are independent of the choice of component
C\subseteq S
.
Thus we aim to convert all double points to being of type (I). Note that both double points introduced when performing a finger move are of type (I).

If
p_{i}
is of type (II), the sign
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
is the same for all components
C
which contain a preimage of
p_{i}
, although it may differ for components
C
which do not contain a preimage of
p_{i}
. As such, we may convert all double points of type (II) to type (I) moves of the third type; that is, by flipping signs of some integers
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
with
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=0
. Thus we henceforth assume all double points are of types (I), (III), or (IV), and that there are no double points of type (II).

If
p_{i}
is of type (III), the signs
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
do depend on the component
C
, but whenever there are two components
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
with
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
, they lie in different choices of
S_{0}
,
S_{1}
, or
S_{2}
. So in particular we are free to perform finger moves between
C
and
C^{\prime}
.

If
p_{i}
is of type (IV), there are choices of components
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
and we are not free to perform finger moves between
C
and
C^{\prime}
.

The procedure for converting double points of types (III) and (IV) to type (I) is relatively simple, though cumbersome to describe, so we recommend the reader consult the diagrammatic summary in
??
. This diagram is to be read as follows. Each shaded region corresponds to a component of
S
, possibly with multiple shaded regions corresponding to the same component. Components are approximately aligned in three columns, corresponding from left-to-right to
S_{0}
,
S_{1}
, and
S_{2}
. Within a component
C
, a node with a sign corresponds to a preimage in
C
of a double point
p_{i}
. The indicated sign is the sign of
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
. Two nodes are joined by an arc if they correspond to the same double point. Thus double points of type (I) are represented by arcs joining two nodes of the same sign; double points of type (III) are arcs connecting nodes of different signs which cross from one column into another; double points of type (IV) are arcs connecting nodes of different signs which stay within a single column.

(IV):
C
C^{\prime}
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
(III):
C

\leadsto

\leadsto

Figure 5.
A diagrammatic representation of how to convert all double points into type (I). Top: example converting from a double point of type (IV) to one of type (III), as well as two of type (I). Bottom: example converting from a pair of double points of type (III) to six of type (I).

The first two moves outlined above are shown on in the diagram as follows. Performing a finger move looks like adding a pair of arcs between two components in different columns, and setting both nodes on one arc to positive and both nodes on the other arc to negative. These new arcs must cross horizontally from one column into another. When we perform this move, the introduced arcs are marked in blue in the diagram.
The second move looks like swapping two different signs of nodes in a component. When we perform this move, the swapped signs are marked in red in the diagram. The third move cannot be represented in the diagram, but we will also not require it again, because we only use it to implicitly convert all double points of type (II) into type (I).

We first describe how to arrange that there are no double points of type (IV); see the top of
??
.
Suppose
p_{i}
is of type (IV), and let
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
be components such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{C}=\mathcal{P}_{i}^{C^{\prime}}=1
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
C,C^{\prime}\subset S_{0}
. By performing a finger move if necessary, we may assume there is some double point
p_{j}
and a component
D\subset S_{1}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{j}=\mathcal{P}^{D}_{j}=1
and
\varepsilon^{C}_{j}=-\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
. By flipping the signs of
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
and
\varepsilon^{C}_{j}
, we may arrange that
p_{i}
is of type (I), at the expense of making
p_{j}
of type (III). Hence we may assume there are no double points of type (IV).

We now describe how to arrange that there are no double points of type (III), assuming that there are none of type (IV); see the bottom of
??
.
Suppose that
p_{i}
is of type (III), and let
C,C^{\prime}\subset S
be components such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}\neq\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{C}=\mathcal{P}_{i}^{C^{\prime}}=1
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
C\subseteq S_{0}
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq S_{1}
. Consider the sum

e(g(S),s^{Z})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{C\subseteq S}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{C}_{i},

which was arranged to be true earlier by the choices of signs
\varepsilon_{i}^{C}
.
Any double point of type (I) contributes
\pm 2
to this sum, while a double point of type (III) contributes 0. So if there are
m
double points of type (III),

2n\equiv e(g(S),s^{Z})\equiv 2(n-m)\mod{4},

where we recall that the first equivalence is by assumption. Therefore
m
must be even. In particular, there must exist another double point
p_{j}
of type (III), say with
\mathcal{P}^{D}_{j}=\mathcal{P}^{D^{\prime}}_{j}=1
and
\varepsilon^{D}_{j}=-\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
, for two components
D,D^{\prime}\subset S
. Find some
k\in\{0,1,2\}
such that
D\subseteq S_{k}
. Then we can find
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,
\ell\neq k
, and a component
F\subseteq S_{\ell}
. By performing finger moves between
C
and
F
and between
D
and
F
if necessary, we can assume there exist double points
p_{s}
,
p_{t}
of type (I) such that

\mathcal{P}^{C}_{s},\mathcal{P}^{F}_{s},\mathcal{P}^{D}_{t},\mathcal{P}^{F}_{t}=1,\text{ and }\varepsilon^{C}_{s}=\varepsilon^{F}_{s}=-\varepsilon^{D}_{t}=-\varepsilon^{F}_{t}=-\varepsilon^{C}_{i}.

By flipping the signs of
\varepsilon^{C}_{s}
,
\varepsilon^{F}_{s}
,
\varepsilon^{D}_{t}
,
\varepsilon^{F}_{t}
,
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
, and
\varepsilon^{D}_{j}
, we convert both
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
to type (I), while keeping
p_{s}
and
p_{t}
as type (I). Hence we can assume there are no double points of type (III).

Hence all double points may be assumed to be of type (I), and we are done by
??
as described above.
∎

If
S=S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}
with each
S_{i}
non-empty, then the regular homotopy from
f
to
g
described in the proof of
??
restricts to an isotopy of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{S_{i}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{S_{i}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{S_{i}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{S_{i}}}
for each
i=0,1,2
. However, if we can only assume that
S=S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}
, such as when
S
is the union of two properly embedded connected surfaces which may intersect,
the conversion from double points of type (III) to type (I) or (II) outlined above requires self-finger moves of either
S_{0}
or
S_{1}
. In fact, an extra condition is required to be able to make
Z
almost-extendable over
\Sigma
after just isotopies of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{S_{0}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{S_{0}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{S_{0}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{S_{0}}}
and
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{S_{1}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{S_{1}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{S_{1}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{S_{1}}}
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
. Suppose that
S=S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}
is a disjoint union of two non-empty compact surfaces. Write
\Sigma_{i}=f(S_{i})
and
T=\self(\Sigma_{0})+\self(\Sigma_{1})
.
Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

There exists an immersion
g\colon S\looparrowright X
regularly homotopic to
f
such that the homotopy restricts to an isotopy on both
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
, and
Z
is almost-extendable over
g(S)
.

(ii)

The class
[Z\cup\Sigma]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and

e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\in\{-2T,-2T+4,\ldots,2T\}.

Proof.

There are two additional things to prove in order to adapt the proof of
??
: first, that condition
e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\in\{-2T,-2T+4,\ldots,2T\}
is necessary for (i) to hold; and second, that it is enough to provide an alternative method of converting double points of type (III) into double points of type (I).

We first show the condition is necessary, and hence that (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). By
??
, we have that
2e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\equiv 2[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma_{1}]\bmod{4}
. Subtracting this from
e(\Sigma,s^{Z})\equiv 2\self(\Sigma)\bmod{4}
, which is necessary by
??
, gives the necessary condition

e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\equiv 2\self(\Sigma)-2[\Sigma_{0}]\cdot[\Sigma_{1}]\equiv 2T\mod{4}.

It remains to show that (i) implies that
\left\lvert e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\right\rvert\leq 2T
. Suppose a suitable immersion
g\colon S\looparrowright X
and values
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
have been found, so that for each component
C\subseteq S
,

e(g(C),s^{Z})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}.

Then since
g
is regularly homotopic to
f
,

\displaystyle e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})

\displaystyle=e(g(S_{0}),s^{Z})-e(g(S_{1}),s^{Z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{C\subseteq S_{0}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{i}-\sum_{C\subseteq S_{1}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}\varepsilon^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n,\\
g^{-1}(p_{i})\subset S_{0}\end{subarray}}2\varepsilon_{i}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n,\\
g^{-1}(p_{i})\subset S_{1}\end{subarray}}2\varepsilon_{i},

where the last equality follows because double points
p_{i}
with preimages in both
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
contribute 0 to the sum. But since the homotopy from
f
to
g
is assumed to be an isotopy on each
S_{i}
, this gives

e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n,\\
f^{-1}(p_{i})\subset S_{0}\end{subarray}}2\varepsilon_{i}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n,\\
f^{-1}(p_{i})\subset S_{1}\end{subarray}}2\varepsilon_{i}.

The triangle inequality then gives that

\left\lvert e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\right\rvert\leq 2\self(\Sigma_{0})+2\self(\Sigma_{1})=2T.

Hence (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii).

We now provide an alternative method of removing double points of type (III), under the assumption that there are no double points of type (II) or (IV). This will prove that (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Again, the procedure is best described diagrammatically as in
??
. This diagram is to be read in the same way as
??
; note, however, that the grey regions corresponding to the different components of
S
are now only arranged into two columns, corresponding to
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
respectively.

(a)
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
(b)
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
G
F
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
G
F
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
G
F
(c)
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
G
F
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
G
F
\leadsto
C
C^{\prime}
D
D^{\prime}
G
F

Figure 6.
A revised schema for converting double points of type (III) to type (I). The three rows (a)–(c) refer to different possible configurations of double points of type (III).

Suppose that
p_{i}
is of type (III), with
\mathcal{P}^{C}_{i}=\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}_{i}=1
and
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}=-\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
C\subseteq S_{0}
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq S_{1}
. As before, there must exist another double point
p_{j}
of type (III), say with
\mathcal{P}^{D}_{j}=\mathcal{P}^{D^{\prime}}_{j}=1
. We can order
D
and
D^{\prime}
such that
D\subseteq S_{0}
and
D^{\prime}\subseteq S_{1}
. We have three cases to consider, each shown in
??
.

(a)

Suppose we can choose
p_{j}
such that
\varepsilon^{C}_{i}=-\varepsilon^{D}_{j}
. Then we are in the situation of
??
(a). By performing a finger move between
C^{\prime}
and
D
if necessary, we may assume that there is a double point
p_{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{D}_{\ell}=1\text{ and }\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell}=-\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}=-\varepsilon^{D}_{i}=\varepsilon^{D}_{\ell}.

Then by flipping the signs of
\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell}
,
\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
,
\varepsilon^{D}_{j}
, and
\varepsilon^{D}_{\ell}
, we convert both
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
to type (I), while keeping
p_{\ell}
type (I).

(b)

Suppose we are not in case (a), but that there is a double point
p_{\ell}
of
g(S_{0})
and a component
F\subseteq S_{0}
with
\mathcal{P}^{F}_{\ell}\geq 1
and
\varepsilon^{F}_{\ell}=-\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
. Let
G\subseteq S_{0}
be the other component of
S_{0}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{G}_{\ell}\geq 1
, noting that possibly
F=G
. Then we are in the situation of
??
(b). Since
p_{\ell}
cannot be of type (IV) by assumption, or type (III) since it is a point of intersection between components in
S_{0}
, it must be of type (I). So
\varepsilon^{F}_{\ell}=\varepsilon^{G}_{\ell}
.

By performing finger moves if necessary, we may assume there are double points
p_{s}
,
p_{t}
of type (I) such that

\mathcal{P}^{F}_{s}=\mathcal{P}^{C^{\prime}}_{s}=\mathcal{P}^{G}_{t}=\mathcal{P}^{D^{\prime}}_{t}=1\text{ and }\varepsilon^{F}_{s}=\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{s}=\varepsilon^{G}_{t}=\varepsilon^{D^{\prime}}_{t}=-\varepsilon^{F}_{\ell}.

By flipping the signs of
\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{i}
,
\varepsilon^{C^{\prime}}_{s}
,
\varepsilon^{D^{\prime}}_{j}
,
\varepsilon^{D^{\prime}}_{t}
,
\varepsilon^{F}_{\ell}
,
\varepsilon^{F}_{s}
,
\varepsilon^{G}_{\ell}
, and
\varepsilon^{G}_{t}
, we convert both
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
to type (I), while keeping each of
p_{\ell}
,
p_{s}
and
p_{t}
as type (I).

Note that if
F=G
, flipping the signs of
\varepsilon^{F}_{\ell}=\varepsilon^{G}_{\ell}
and
\varepsilon^{F}_{s}=\varepsilon^{G}_{s}
is slightly different to the move described in
??
, since
\mathcal{P}^{F}_{\ell}=2
. However, it is still a legal move, since it still does not affect
\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{F}_{i}\varepsilon^{F}_{i}
.

(c)

Suppose that we are not in case (a) or (b). Then consider the sum

e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{C\subseteq S_{0}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{k}\varepsilon^{C}_{k}-\sum_{C\subseteq S_{1}}\mathcal{P}^{C}_{k}\varepsilon^{C}_{k}\right).

Since we are not in case (a), we may assume that all double points of type (III) contribute
2\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
to the sum. A double point of type (I) contributes 0 to the sum if it is not a double point of
g(S_{0})
or
g(S_{1})
. If it is a double point of
g(S_{0})
, it must contribute
2\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
to the sum, since we are not in case (b). If all double points of
g(S_{1})
contributed
2\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
to the sum, then we would have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=2\cdot\#\{\text{double points of type (III)}\}+2\self(g(S_{0}))+2\self(g(S_{1}))

\displaystyle\geq 4+2T,

which contradicts the assumption that
\left\lvert e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})-e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})\right\rvert\leq 2T
. So there must be a double point
p_{\ell}
of
g(S_{1})
and a component
F\subseteq S_{1}
with
\mathcal{P}^{F}_{\ell}\geq 1
and
\varepsilon^{F}_{\ell}=\varepsilon^{C}_{i}
. This case proceeds exactly as case (b), but reversing the roles of
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
. See
??
(c).

We can therefore arrange that there are no double points of type (III). Hence all double points are of type (I), and we are done.
∎

We can then deduce
??
from
??
, since
\self(\Sigma_{0})=\self(\Sigma_{1})=0
when
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
are embedded in
X
. We restate the conclusion for the readers convenience.

Theorem

(
??
)
.

Let
X
be an orientable 4-manifold, and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be two properly embedded compact surfaces. Let
Z\subset\partial X
be a spanning surface for
\partial\Sigma_{0}\cup\partial\Sigma_{1}
with associated Seifert section
s^{Z}
. Then there are surfaces
\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
isotopic to
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
respectively such that
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
if and only if
[\Sigma_{0}\cup Z\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})=e(\Sigma_{1},s^{Z})
.

6.
Cobordisms of properly embedded surfaces

In this section we will prove our main results on cobordism.
In
??
, we prove the oriented Theorems
1
and
1
using the techniques developed in
??
. These are very similar to classical results in
[
31
]
, though relax the conditions that the ambient manifold needs to be closed. We prove Theorem
D
, Theorem
E
and Corollary
F
in Sections
6.2
,
6.3
, and
6.4
respectively. Throughout, we write
\pr_{X}\colon X\times I\to X
for the projection map onto the ambient manifold
X
.

6.1.
Existence of oriented cobordisms

We can use
??
to directly construct cobordisms between
\mathbb{Z}
-homologous oriented properly embedded codimension 2 submanifolds. This applies in all dimensions.

Recall that we say a cobordism
Z\subset\partial X\times I
from
\partial\Sigma_{0}
to
\partial\Sigma_{1}
extends to a cobordism
Y\subset X\times I
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
if
Y\cap(\partial X\times I)=Z
. If
Y
and
Z
are oriented cobordisms, we require that
Z
has the orientation restricted from
\partial Y
. We first prove
??
, since it is the most different to results already found in the literature.

Theorem

(
??
)
.

Let
X
be a connected oriented
(n+2)
-manifold and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be oriented properly embedded compact
n
-manifolds.
Let
Z\subset\partial X\times I
be an oriented cobordism from
\partial\Sigma_{0}
to
\partial\Sigma_{1}
. Then
Z
extends to an oriented cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
if and only if

[-\Sigma_{0}\cup-\pr_{X}(Z)\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

Write
B\coloneq-\Sigma_{0}\mathord{\times}\{0\}\cup-Z\cup\Sigma_{1}\mathord{\times}\{1\}\subset\partial(X\mathord{\times}I).

The forward direction is clear, since if
B
is the boundary of some oriented cobordism in
X\times I
, then
[B]=0\in H_{n}(X\times I;\mathbb{Z})
, which is equivalent to
[\pr_{X}(B)]=0\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

To show the reverse, suppose that
[B]=0\in H_{n}(X\times I;\mathbb{Z})
. By considering the exact sequence of the pair
(X\times I,\partial(X\times I))
, there exists some
\beta\in H_{n+1}(X\times I,\partial(X\times I);\mathbb{Z})
such that
\partial\beta=[B]\in H_{n}(\partial(X\times I);\mathbb{Z})
.
Since
Z\subset\partial X\times I
and
-\Sigma_{0}\mathord{\times}\{0\}\cup\Sigma_{1}\mathord{\times}\{1\}\subset X\times\partial I
both meet
\partial X\times\partial I
transversely, we can smooth the corners of
X\times I
so that
B
is smoothly embedded in
\partial(X\times I)
. We can then apply
??
with
M=X\times I
and
A=\partial(X\times I)
to find an oriented cobordism
Y
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
extending
Z
.
∎

Similar methods can also be used to prove
??
, where the cobordism
Z
on the boundary is not prescribed.

Theorem

(
??
)
.

Let
X
be a connected oriented
(n+2)
-manifold and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be oriented properly embedded compact
n
-manifolds. Then there is an oriented cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
if and only if
[\Sigma_{0}]=[\Sigma_{1}]\in H_{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

The forward direction is clear. The reverse direction follows almost exactly as in
??
, except that
??
is applied with with
M=X\times I
,
A=X\times\{0,1\}
, and
B=-\Sigma_{0}\mathord{\times}\{0\}\cup\Sigma_{1}\mathord{\times}\{1\}
.
∎

In the unoriented case, this approach fails even for
n=2
, since we do not have a suitable analogue of
??
for finding compact 3-manifolds representing
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-homology classes in 5-manifolds.

6.2.
Proof of Theorem
D

Instead of using the classical approaches of
??
, we build unoriented cobordisms between surfaces by “stretching out” spanning 3-manifolds away from the intersection points between the surfaces. More precisely, suppose we have properly embedded compact surfaces
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
, and a spanning manifold
Z\subset\partial X
for
\partial\Sigma_{0}\sqcup\partial\Sigma_{1}
.
Suppose that
Z
is almost-extendable over
\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
, in the sense of
??
. That is, write
\widehat{\Sigma}\subset\widehat{X}
for the image of the punctured embedding of the immersion given by the union of the two embeddings
\Sigma_{0}\hookrightarrow X
and
\Sigma_{1}\hookrightarrow X
; then we are supposing that we can find a spanning 3-manifold
\widehat{Y}\subset\widehat{X}
for
\widehat{\Sigma}
, which meets each component of
\partial\widehat{X}\setminus\partial X
in an annulus. If this is the case,
\widehat{Y}
can be stretched out into
\widehat{X}\times I
, and these annuli stretch out into the trace of an isotopy in
\partial\widehat{X}\setminus\partial X
from
\Sigma_{0}\cap(\partial\widehat{X}\setminus\partial X)
to
\Sigma_{1}\cap(\partial\widehat{X}\setminus\partial X)
. This trace can be “filled in” to give a cobordism in
X\times I
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
by the following lemma.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
X=D^{4}
, let
\Sigma_{0}=D^{2}\times\{(0,0)\}
, and let
\Sigma_{1}=\{(0,0)\}\times D^{2}
. Let
A\subset\partial D^{4}
be an annulus spanning the Hopf link
\partial\Sigma_{0}\sqcup\partial\Sigma_{1}
. Fix an identification
\varphi\colon A\to S^{1}\times I
such that
\varphi^{-1}(S^{1}\times\{i\})=\partial\Sigma_{i}
for
i=0,1
. Let
\pr_{I}\colon S^{1}\times I\to I
be the projection map, and let

Z\coloneq\{(a,\pr_{I}\varphi(a))\mid a\in A\}\subset\partial D^{4}\times I.

Then there is a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
extending
Z
.

Proof.

The map
S^{1}\times I\to\partial D^{4}\times I
given by
(x,t)\mapsto(\varphi^{-1}(x,t),t)
is an isotopy from
\partial\Sigma_{0}
to
\partial\Sigma_{1}
with trace
Z
. This can be extended radially to a map
F\colon D^{2}\times I\to D^{4}\times I
given by

F(\lambda\cdot x,t)=(\lambda\varphi^{-1}(x,t),t)\quad\text{for $x\in S^{1}$, $\lambda\in[0,1]$, $t\in I$.}

Then
F
is an isotopy from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
which fixes the intersection point at the origin.
Hence
F(D^{2}\times I)\subset D^{4}\times I
is a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
extending
Z
.
∎

We have now developed all of the necessary technical tools to prove Theorem
D
.

Theorem

(Theorem
D
)
.

Let
X
be a connected orientable 4-manifold, and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be properly embedded compact surfaces.
Let
Z\subset\partial X\times I
be a cobordism from
\partial\Sigma_{0}
to
\partial\Sigma_{1}
.
Then
Z
extends to a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
if and only if
[\Sigma_{0}\cup\pr_{X}(Z)\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
e(\Sigma_{1},s_{1})=e(\Sigma_{0},s_{0})+e(Z,s_{0}\cup s_{1})
after an arbitrary choice of framing
s_{i}
of
\partial\Sigma_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

Proof.

We first prove the forward direction. Let
Y\subset X\times I
be a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
extending
Z
. The homological condition is necessary, since

[\Sigma_{0}\cup\pr_{X}(Z)\cup\Sigma_{1}]=[\pr_{X}(\partial Y)]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

Recall that, after fixing an orientation on
X
,

\partial(X\times I)=-X\mathord{\times}\{0\}\cup-\partial X\mathord{\times}I\cup X\mathord{\times}\{1\}.

So
??
of the relative Euler numbers by counting intersection points with a push-off shows that

e(\partial Y)=-e(\Sigma_{0},s_{0})-e(Z,s_{0}\cup s_{1})+e(\Sigma_{1},s_{1}),

for any choices of framings
s_{i}
of the link
\partial\Sigma_{i}
.
But
e(\partial Y)=0
by
??
, hence the forward direction follows.

We now prove the reverse direction. Note that it suffices to consider the case where
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
are both non-empty, since the empty submanifold is cobordant to a trivial 2-sphere, which is non-empty. We may also assume that
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
only intersect transversely. In particular, this means that
\partial\Sigma_{0}
and
\partial\Sigma_{1}
are disjoint, and that
\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
is the image of a generic proper immersion
f\colon S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}\looparrowright X
of a compact surface such that
f(S_{i})=\Sigma_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

We first consider the case where
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
are closed and
Z=\varnothing
. We apply
??
. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}\subset\widehat{X}
be the image of the punctured embedding of
f
, and let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\subset X
be the 4-balls such that
