\widehat{X}=\overline{X\setminus(D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{n})}
.
??
says that, potentially after isotopies of
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
, there is a spanning manifold
\widehat{Y}
for
\widehat{\Sigma}
such that
A_{j}\coloneq\widehat{Y}\cap\partial D_{j}
is an annulus for each
j=1,\ldots,n
.

Write
\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}\coloneq\Sigma_{i}\cap\widehat{\Sigma}
for
i=0,1
.
Fix identifications
\varphi_{j}\colon A_{j}\to S^{1}\times I
such that

\varphi_{j}^{-1}(S^{1}\times\{i\})=A_{j}\cap\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}\quad\text{for $i=0,1$ and $j=1,\ldots,n$.}

Then by Tietze’s extension theorem and smooth approximation, there is a map
F\colon\widehat{Y}\to I
such that

F^{-1}(\{i\})=\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}\text{ for $i=0,1$}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathchoice{{F\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{A_{j}}}{{F\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{A_{j}}}{{F\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{A_{j}}}{{F\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{A_{j}}}=\pr_{I}\varphi_{j}\colon A_{j}\to I\text{ for $j=1,\ldots,n$.}

where
\pr_{I}\colon S^{1}\times I\to I
is the projection map.
Let
G\colon\widehat{Y}\to\widehat{X}\times I
be the embedding given by
G(y)=(y,F(y))
, so that the image
G(\widehat{Y})\subset\widehat{X}\times I
is a cobordism from
\widehat{\Sigma}_{0}
to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}
.

But by applying
??
to each
D_{j}
, we can find cobordisms
Y_{j}\subset D_{j}\times I
from
\Sigma_{0}\cap D_{j}
to
\Sigma_{1}\cap D_{j}
extending
G(\widehat{Y}\cap D_{j})=\{(a,\pr_{I}\varphi_{j}a)\mid a\in A_{j}\}
for each
j=1,\ldots,n
.
Then

Y\coloneq G(\widehat{Y})\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}Y_{j}\subset X\times I

is a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
. We have therefore shown the result in the case that
Z=\varnothing
.

Now consider the general case, where
\partial\Sigma_{0},\partial\Sigma_{1}\subset\partial X
and
Z\subset\partial X\times I
may be non-empty. Let

\Sigma^{\prime}\coloneq\Sigma_{0}\mathord{\times}\{0\}\cup Z\cup\Sigma_{1}\mathord{\times}\{1\}\subset\partial(X\times I).

We again smooth the corners of
X\times I
so that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a closed surface smoothly embedded in
\partial(X\times I)
. Consider the long exact sequence of the pair
(X\times I,\partial(X\times I))
. Since
[\Sigma^{\prime}]=0\in H_{2}(X\times I;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
by assumption, exactness says that there exists a class
\alpha\in H_{3}(X\times I,\partial(X\times I);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
such that
\partial\alpha=[\Sigma]\in H_{2}(\partial(X\times I);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. But there is a suspension isomorphism

H_{2}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{3}(X\times I,\partial(X\times I);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})

given by
[\sigma]\mapsto[\sigma\times I]
, so by
??
, there is a properly embedded compact surface
M\subset X
such that
[\Sigma^{\prime}]=[\partial(M\times I)]\in H_{2}(\partial(X\times I);{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Furthermore,
e(\partial(M\times I))=0
by
??
. Since
e(\Sigma^{\prime})=0
by assumption, the closed case done above gives a cobordism
Y^{\prime}\subset\partial(X\times I)\times I
from
\Sigma^{\prime}
to
\partial(M\times I)
.

Finally, let
(C,\varphi)
be a collar of
\partial(X\times I)
, and fix an identification
\theta\colon X\times I\to\overline{(X\times I)\setminus C}
such that
\theta(x)=\varphi(x,1)
for
x\in\partial(X\times I)
. Then

Y\coloneq\varphi(Y^{\prime})\mathop{\cup}_{\theta(\partial(M\times I))}\theta(M\times I)

is the required cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
extending
Z
.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
E

In this subsection we prove Theorem
E
. We first state a lemma.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
Y
be a closed orientable 3-manifold and let
L_{0},L_{1}\subset Y
be two disjoint embedded closed 1-manifolds. Let
Z\subset Y\times I
be a cobordism from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
. Let
\pr_{Y}\colon Z\to Y
and
\pr_{I}\colon Z\to I
be the restrictions of projection maps from
Y\times I
. Assume that
{\pr_{Y}}
is an embedding near
\partial Z
, and hence determines an associated section
s^{Z}\colon L_{0}\cup L_{1}\to SN_{Y}{(L_{0}\cup L_{1})}
by taking the normal direction into
\pr_{Y}(Z)
.
If
{\pr_{Y}}\colon Z\looparrowright Y
is an immersion, then
e(Z,s^{Z})=0
.

Proof.

If
\pr_{Y}
is an immersion, then it has a normal bundle
N(\pr_{Y})
, giving a quotient map

q\colon N_{Y\times I}Z=\frac{\pr_{Y}^{*}TY\oplus\pr_{I}^{*}TI}{TZ}\to\frac{\pr_{Y}^{*}TY}{TZ}=N(\pr_{Y}).

This is surjective, so in particular of constant rank, giving a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{Y\times I}Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\pr_{Y})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

of vector bundles over
Z
.
Since orientation characters are additive under extension and both
Y
and
I
are orientable,
N_{Y\times I}Z
and
N(\pr_{Y})
both have the same orientation character as
TZ
. Thus the orientation character of
K
is trivial, and since
K
is a line bundle,
K
is trivial.

After identifying
\partial Z
with its image
L_{0}\cup L_{1}
, the bundle
\mathchoice{{N(\pr_{Y})\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{N(\pr_{Y})\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{N(\pr_{Y})\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{11.49998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{10.6389pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{N(\pr_{Y})\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{10.55553pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{9.69444pt}}_{\partial Z}}
describes the directions normal to both
L_{0}\cup L_{1}
and
\pr_{Y}(Z)
. Thus
\mathchoice{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{\partial Z}}
describes the direction normal to
L_{0}\cup L_{1}
but tangent to
\pr_{Y}(Z)
. Hence
\mathchoice{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{\partial Z}}
is the line subbundle spanned by
s^{Z}
. Since
\mathchoice{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{\partial Z}}{{K\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{\partial Z}}
extends to the trivial line bundle
K
over
Z
, the section
s^{Z}
extends to a non-vanishing section of
N_{Y\times I}Z
over all of
Z
.
∎

We now deduce Theorem
E
.

Theorem

(Theorem
E
)
.

Let
X
be a connected orientable 4-manifold and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be properly embedded compact surfaces. Then
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
are cobordant if and only if
[\Sigma_{0}]=[\Sigma_{1}]\in H_{2}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and either
\partial X\neq\varnothing
or
e(\Sigma_{0})=e(\Sigma_{1})
.

Proof.

If
\partial X=\varnothing
, then the statement of Theorem
E
is exactly Theorem
D
. So suppose that
\partial X\neq\varnothing
. Then the forward direction is clear by the same argument as Theorem
D
.

So we prove the reverse direction, and suppose that
[\Sigma_{0}]=[\Sigma_{1}]\in H_{2}(X,\partial X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
\partial X\neq\varnothing
. Let
L\coloneq\partial\Sigma_{0}\cup\partial\Sigma_{1}
, and consider the long exact sequence of the triple
(X,\partial X,L)
. Since
[\Sigma_{0}]
and
[\Sigma_{1}]
are homologous, by exactness there is some
\alpha\in H_{2}(\partial X,L;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
whose image in
H_{2}(X,L;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
under inclusion is precisely
[\Sigma_{0}]+[\Sigma_{1}]
. By applying
??
to
L
, there exists some spanning manifold
Z^{\prime}\subset\partial X
for
L
such that
[Z^{\prime}]=\alpha\in H_{2}(\partial X,L;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. In particular then
[\Sigma_{0}\cup Z^{\prime}\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X,L;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Since
H_{2}(L;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})=0
, this implies that
[\Sigma_{0}\cup Z^{\prime}\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
by the long exact sequence of the pair
(X,L)
.

Let
f\colon Z^{\prime}\to I
be any map with
f^{-1}(\{i\})=\partial\Sigma_{i}
for
i=0,1
, and let

Z=\big\{(z,f(z))\mid z\in Z^{\prime}\big\}\subset\partial X\times I.

Note that
Z
is a cobordism from
\partial\Sigma_{0}
to
\partial\Sigma_{1}
, and the projection
\partial X\times I\to\partial X
restricts to an embedding on
Z
. Let
s
be the Seifert section associated to
Z^{\prime}
. Then by
??
,
e(Z,s)=0
. But by
??
,
e(\Sigma_{0},s)\equiv e(\Sigma_{1},s)\bmod{2}
, and hence

e(\Sigma_{0}\cup Z\cup\Sigma_{1})\equiv-e(\Sigma_{0},s)-e(Z,s)+e(\Sigma_{1},s)\equiv 0\mod{2}.

Since an unknotted
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset D^{4}
has
e(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2})=\pm 2
by
[
33
]
, we can let
M\subset\partial X\times I
be a disjoint union of copies of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, disjoint from
Z
, such that
e(M)=-e(\Sigma_{0}\cup Z\cup\Sigma_{1})
. Hence by Theorem
D
, there is a cobordism
Y
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
extending
Z\cup M
.
∎

6.4.
Proof of Corollary
F

We now consider the situation where
\partial\Sigma_{0}=\partial\Sigma_{1}
, and prove Corollary
F
. Recall that we call a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
a cobordism rel. boundary if it extends
\partial\Sigma_{0}\times I
.

Theorem

(Corollary
F
)
.

Let
X
be an orientable 4-manifold and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\subset X
be properly embedded compact surfaces such that
\partial\Sigma_{0}=\partial\Sigma_{1}
. Then
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
are cobordant rel. boundary if and only if
[\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
and
e(\Sigma_{0},s)=e(\Sigma_{1},s)
after an arbitrary choice of framing
s
of
\partial\Sigma_{0}
.

Proof.

Write
L\coloneq\partial\Sigma_{0}=\partial\Sigma_{1}
. We wish to apply
??
with
L_{i}=\partial\Sigma_{i}
for
i=0,1
, but they are not disjoint links. We therefore first perturb
\Sigma_{1}
to a surface
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
which is transverse to
\Sigma_{1}
. We do this as follows. Fix a section
s^{\prime}\colon\Sigma_{1}\to DN_{X}\Sigma_{1}
which is transverse to the zero section, such that
\mathchoice{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{12.32336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{11.46228pt}}_{L}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.82336pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.96228pt}}_{L}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{10.10114pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{9.24005pt}}_{L}}{{s^{\prime}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{8.52777pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{7.66669pt}}_{L}}\colon L\to\mathchoice{{SN_{X}\Sigma_{1}\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{L}}{{SN_{X}\Sigma_{1}\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{L}}{{SN_{X}\Sigma_{1}\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.09444pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.23335pt}}_{L}}{{SN_{X}\Sigma_{1}\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.41666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.55557pt}}_{L}}
represents the framing
s
. Fix a tubular neighbourhood
(\nu\Sigma_{1},\varphi)
of
\Sigma_{1}
, and let
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}\coloneq\varphi s(\Sigma_{1})
be the push-off of
\Sigma_{1}
in the direction of
s^{\prime}
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}\coloneq\partial\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
are disjoint, and hence we may assume that
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
is transverse to both
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
.

Let
g\colon\Sigma_{1}\times I\to X\times I
be the isotopy given by
g(x,t)=(\varphi(t\cdot s(x)),t)
, which takes
\Sigma_{1}
to
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
. Let
Z\coloneq g(L\times I)\subset\partial X\times I
, so that
Z
is a cobordism from
L
to
L^{\prime}
. Extend
g
to an ambient isotopy
G\colon X\times I\to X\times I
. Then there is a cobordism rel. boundary
Y
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
if and only if there is a cobordism
G(Y)
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
extending
Z
.

Let
s^{Z}
be the Seifert section associated to
Z
. Then by
??
,
e(Z,s^{Z})=0
. Since
\mathchoice{{s^{Z}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{13.2178pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{12.35672pt}}_{L}}{{s^{Z}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{12.7178pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{11.85672pt}}_{L}}{{s^{Z}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{10.74002pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{9.87894pt}}_{L}}{{s^{Z}\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.16666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.30557pt}}_{L}}=s
, we have that
e(\Sigma_{0},s^{Z})=e(\Sigma_{0},s)
. Also
e(\Sigma^{\prime}_{1},s^{Z})=e(\Sigma_{1},s)
, since both are given by counting the intersections between
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\Sigma_{1}
as in
??
, with signs coming from a choice of orientation of
X
. Thus by Theorem
D
, there is a cobordism from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
extending
Z
if and only if

[\Sigma_{0}\cup\pr_{X}(Z)\cup\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}]=[\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}]=0\in H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}),

and
e(\Sigma_{0},s)=e(\Sigma_{1},s)
. This proves the result.
∎

7.
Ambient surgery

For the remainder of this paper, our focus will shift from constructing cobordisms to constructing concordances. Our primary technique for proving the existence of a concordance between two connected surfaces in a simply-connected 4-manifold
X
will be to surger an arbitrary connected cobordism
Y\subset X\times I
between them into a concordance.
This approach dates back to Kervaire, who used it to show that any two embedded
2k
-spheres in
S^{2k+2}
are concordant
[
16
]
. Our approach will be heavily based on the approach of Sunukjian in
[
30
]
and the exposition by Klug–Miller in
[
21
]
.

In this section, we review ambient surgery on embedded submanifolds, recalling or proving several key lemmas. All material found here is based on similar material in
[
30
]
and
[
21
]
, but is recorded here for ease of referencing. Some results are also extended or have details added. In places, our notation differs; in particular, what we refer to as
k
-surgery (referencing the dimension of the
k
-sphere) is referred to as
(k+1)
-surgery (referencing the dimension of the
(k+1)
-handle) by both Sunukjian and Klug–Miller. We make this change to agree with the standard convention in surgery theory (c.f.
[
27
]
,
[
22
]
).

7.1.
Ambient surgery

Let
X
be a connected
n
-manifold, let
Y\subset X
be a properly embedded compact
m
-manifold, and let
K\subset Y
be an embedded
k
-sphere with
N_{Y}K
trivial.
A
(k+1)
-disc
\Delta\subset X
is said to
span
K
into
X\setminus Y
if it is embedded in
X
, meets
Y
transversely, and
\partial\Delta=\Delta\cap Y=K
.
Following
[
21
]
, a framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
is called
\Delta
-admissible
if it extends to a framing of some trivial subbundle
\xi\subseteq N_{X}\Delta
of rank
m-k
. Then a choice of tubular neighbourhood
(\nu\Delta,\eta)
determines the submanifold with corners

Y^{\prime}\coloneq\overline{Y\setminus\eta(DN_{Y}K)}\mathop{\cup}_{\eta(SN_{Y}K)}\eta(S\xi)\subseteq X.

After smoothing corners, we say that
Y^{\prime}
is the result of
ambient
k
-surgery over
\Delta
with framing
\varphi
. Note that
Y^{\prime}
is a proper embedding of the result of abstract surgery along
K
with framing
\varphi
, and is determined by
\Delta
and
\varphi
up to isotopy.

The same framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
may be
\Delta
-admissible for some choices of
(k+1)
-disc
\Delta
but not others. An abstract surgery along a framed
k
-sphere can be realised ambiently in
X
if and only if the framing is
\Delta
-admissible for some choice of disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
.

Ambient surgery can also be viewed in terms of cobordism.
Let
Y\times I\cup h^{k+1}
be the trace of some abstract
k
-surgery on
Y
, where
h^{k+1}
is the
(m+1)
-dimensional
(k+1)
-handle determining the surgery. This surgery can be realised ambiently if and only if the embedding
Y\times I\subset X\times I
can be extended to an embedding of the trace of surgery with
h^{k+1}\subset X\times\{1\}
. It can be realised over
\Delta
if and only if the image of core of
h^{k+1}
can be taken to be
\Delta\times\{1\}
.

By considering handle decompositions and the “rising water principle”
[
13
, §6.2]
, one can see that the fundamental group of the complement
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is only affected by ambient surgery with either very small or very large codimension. The proof of this is identical to that of Proposition 4.1 in
[
30
]
for the case
n=m+2
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
2\leq m-k\leq n-3
. If
Y^{\prime}
is obtained by ambient
k
-surgery on
Y\subset X
, then
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)\cong\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y^{\prime})
.

We will mostly consider the case where
Y
has codimension 2; that is,
n=m+2
. Then only ambient 0-surgery (that is, ambient connected sum) and ambient
(m-1)
-surgery affect
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
.
If
X
is simply-connected, then
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is normally generated by meridians to
Y
. By performing ambient 0-surgery along some arc, the meridians at the endpoints of the arc get identified in
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
. After choosing a set of meridians normally generating
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
and identifying them in this way, we get the following result.

Lemma
\thetheorem

(
[
30
, Proposition 4.3]
)
.

Suppose that
n=m+2
and that
X
is simply-connected. Then there exists a properly embedded compact
m
-manifold
Y^{\prime}\subset X
obtained from
Y
by performing finitely many ambient 0-surgeries to
Y
, such that
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y^{\prime})
is cyclic and generated by a meridian to
Y^{\prime}
. We can take
Y^{\prime}
to be orientable if and only if
Y
is.

The condition that
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is cyclic and generated by a meridian to
Y
will be especially useful, since it guarantees that every curve
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to Y
has a null-homotopic push-off in
X\setminus Y
. This is because, after choosing an arbitrary push-off, it can be altered by meridians to
Y
until it is null-homotopic, but up to isotopy this is equivalent to simply changing the choice of push-off.

7.2.
Ambient integral Dehn surgery

We specialise to the case
n=5
,
m=3
,
k=1
. That is, we let
X
be a 5-manifold and let
Y\subset X
be a properly embedded compact 3-manifold. This is the case when
Y
is a cobordism between compact surfaces properly embedded in a 4-manifold. We want to perform ambient 1-surgery (i.e. ambient integral Dehn surgery) along a knot
K\subset Y
with trivial normal bundle.

In this case, there is a
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-valued obstruction for a given abstract surgery to be realisable ambiently over a given 2-disc.

Lemma
\thetheorem

(
[
21
, Lemma 5.4]
)
.

Fix a framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
and a 2-disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
. Then the
\Delta
-admissible framings of
N_{Y}K
are exactly the result of either the odd integers or the even integers acting on
\varphi
.

Here the action of
\mathbb{Z}
on the framings of
N_{Y}K
is given by
\star
as in
??
, after identifying framings of
N_{Y}K
with isotopy classes of sections in the usual way. This is only defined up to sign without a choice of orientation on
N_{Y}K
, but it is still possible to define the orbit of
\varphi
under
2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}=N_{\Delta}K\oplus TK\oplus N_{Y}K\oplus\zeta
, where
\zeta
is the line subbundle normal to both
Y
and
\Delta
. See
??
for a schematic.

N_{Y}K
N_{\Delta}K
TK
\zeta
\mathchoice{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
\mathchoice{{T\Delta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{T\Delta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{T\Delta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{T\Delta\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
\mathchoice{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.09444pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.23335pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.41666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.55557pt}}_{K}}
\mathchoice{{N_{X}Y\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}Y\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}Y\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.09444pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.23335pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}Y\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.41666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.55557pt}}_{K}}

Figure 7.
Schematic of the bundle
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
. The four smaller subbundles
N_{Y}K
,
TK
,
N_{\Delta}K
, and
\zeta
represented by arrows are all normal to each other. The number of heads on the arrow indicates the rank. The subbundle spanned by a pair of adjacent subbundles is indicated between them.

Since both
N_{X}\Delta
and
N_{Y}K
are orientable and all orientable vector bundles over
S^{1}
are trivial,
\zeta
must be trivial. Hence the framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
induces a framing of
\mathchoice{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.09444pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.23335pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.41666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.55557pt}}_{K}}=N_{Y}K\oplus\zeta
, which extends over
\Delta
if and only if it is the restriction of the unique framing of
N_{X}\Delta
.

Recall that the framings of a trivial rank
r
bundle over
S^{1}
form a
\pi_{1}(\SO(r))
-torsor. Any Lie subgroup inclusion
\SO(2)\hookrightarrow\SO(3)
induces the quotient map

\pi_{1}(\SO(2))\cong\mathbb{Z}\to{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}\cong\pi_{1}(\SO(3)),

so two framings of the rank 2 bundle
N_{Y}K
induce the same framing of the rank 3 bundle
\mathchoice{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.51385pt]{0.0pt}{12.63885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.65277pt]{0.0pt}{11.77777pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.01941pt]{0.0pt}{10.09444pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.15833pt]{0.0pt}{9.23335pt}}_{K}}{{N_{X}\Delta\rule[-4.7083pt]{0.0pt}{9.41666pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.84721pt]{0.0pt}{8.55557pt}}_{K}}
if and only if they differ by the action of an even integer.
∎

Remark
\thetheorem
.

For more general
m
and
k
with
n=m+2
, the
\Delta
-admissible framings are one orbit class of framings under the action of the kernel

\ker\big(\pi_{k-1}(\SO(m-k+1))\to\pi_{k-1}(\SO(m-k+2))\big)

where the map is induced by Lie subgroup inclusion. For
m\geq 2k
, this map can be seen to be an isomorphism by considering the fibration
\SO(m-k+1)\hookrightarrow\SO(m-k+2)\to S^{m-k+1}
; so in this case, each choice of disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
has a unique framing that is
\Delta
-admissible.

We will find it useful to view
??
from the perspective of stable framings. Let
\xi\colon E\to X
be a trivial vector bundle, and let
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{R}\times X\to X
be the trivial line bundle. We say that two framings of
\xi
are
stably equivalent
if they induce the same framing on
\xi\oplus\ell\varepsilon
for sufficiently large
\ell\geq 0
, and a
stable framing
of
\xi
is a stable equivalence of framings. For
r\geq 3
, the map
\pi_{1}(\SO(r))\to\pi_{1}(\SO(r+1))
induced by Lie group inclusion is an isomorphism between cyclic groups of order 2, so there are two stable framings of a trivial vector bundle over
S^{1}
. So if
\rank(\xi)\geq 3
the framings and stable framings of
\xi
are in bijection; if
\rank(\xi)=2
, a stable framing of
\xi
can be thought of as a framing “mod 2”.
??
says that, for a fixed 2-disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
, the
\Delta
-admissible framings of
N_{Y}K
form one stable equivalence class, since
\varphi
and
1\star\varphi
are representatives of the two distinct stable framings for any framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
.

The following lemma is a simple but useful consequence of naturality of Stiefel–Whitney classes, and the fact that an orientable vector bundle of rank at least 3 on a closed surface is trivial if and only if its second Stiefel–Whitney class vanishes.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
M
be a smooth orientable
n
-manifold with
n\geq 3
, let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface, and let
f\colon\Sigma\to M
be a continuous map. Then
\left\langle w_{2}(M),f_{*}[\Sigma]\right\rangle=0
if and only if
f^{*}TM
is a trivial bundle.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram with exact rows, coming from naturality of the universal coefficient theorem.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom(H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}),{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\Sigma;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom(H_{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism as it is a surjection and both groups are cyclic of order 2.
We consider what it means for the image of
w_{2}(M)\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
to be zero under the diagonal of the square.

•

Going anticlockwise around the square,
w_{2}(M)
maps to
f^{*}w_{2}(M)=w_{2}(f^{*}TM)
, which is zero if and only if
f^{*}TM
is trivial.

•

Going clockwise around the square,
w_{2}(M)
maps to

f^{*}\left\langle w_{2}(M),-\right\rangle=\left\langle w_{2}(M),f_{*}(-)\right\rangle\colon H_{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\to{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}

is the zero map if and only if it is zero on the fundamental class
[\Sigma]
.

By commutativity of the square, these two conditions must coincide.
∎

??
allows Sunukjian to give a condition in terms of the action of
w_{2}(X\setminus Y)
on
H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
which guarantees that all abstract 1-surgeries on
Y
can be performed ambiently in
X

[
30
, Theorem 6.1]
. We repeat the proof here for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
X
is orientable, that
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is cyclic and generated by a meridian to
Y
, and that there exists some
\sigma\in H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
with
\left\langle w_{2}(X\setminus Y),\sigma\right\rangle=1\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
.
Then any abstract 1-surgery on
Y
can be performed ambiently.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is cyclic, the homology group
H_{2}(\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y);\mathbb{Z})=0
, and so the Hurewicz map
\pi_{2}(X\setminus Y)\to H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
is surjective
[
5
, §I.6 & §II.5]
. Hence every class in
H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
is represented by the image of a 2-sphere under a continuous map, which we may assume to be a smooth embedding since
\dim(X)\geq 5
.

Let
\sigma\in H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
be a class such that
\left\langle w_{2}(X\setminus Y),\sigma\right\rangle=1
, and let
\Sigma\subset X\setminus Y
be an oriented embedded 2-sphere with
[\Sigma]=\sigma\in H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
. By
??
, the bundle
\mathchoice{{T(X\setminus Y)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\Sigma}}{{T(X\setminus Y)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{\Sigma}}{{T(X\setminus Y)\rule[-4.76385pt]{0.0pt}{11.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.90277pt]{0.0pt}{10.44446pt}}_{\Sigma}}{{T(X\setminus Y)\rule[-4.26385pt]{0.0pt}{9.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.40277pt]{0.0pt}{8.44446pt}}_{\Sigma}}
must be non-trivial. Since
TS^{2}
is stably trivial, the classification of vector bundles on closed surfaces implies that
N_{X\setminus Y}\Sigma
must be the unique non-trivial orientable bundle of rank 3 over
\Sigma
.

Fix any embedded circle
K\subset Y
with
N_{Y}K
trivial, and any framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
. As
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is generated by a meridian to
Y
,
K
has null-homotopic push-off. Thus there exists a 2-disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
. If
\varphi
is
\Delta
-admissible, we are done. If not, tube
\Delta
to
\Sigma
, to form a new disc
\Delta^{\prime}
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
. Since
\Sigma
has non-trivial normal bundle, the framings of
N_{Y}K
which extend over a subbundle of
N_{X}\Delta
cannot extend over a subbundle of
N_{X}\Delta^{\prime}
, and vice versa. So
\varphi
must be
\Delta^{\prime}
-admissible. Thus we can perform surgery along
K
with framing
\varphi
, either over
\Delta
or over
\Delta^{\prime}
.
∎

Recall that by
??
, when
X
is simply-connected we can always perform ambient 0-surgery to assume that
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is cyclic and generated by a meridian to
Y
. Hence after performing appropriate 0-surgeries, the only obstruction to being able to perform ambient integral Dehn surgery arises when the action of
w_{2}(X\setminus Y)
on
H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
is trivial. In this case, we will use
\Pin^{-}
-structures to control which abstract surgeries can be realised ambiently.

8.
Pin
±
-structures and Pin
±
-surgery

In this section, we extend many of the results about
\Spin
-surgery found in
[
30
]
to its unoriented analogue,
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery. We begin with some important background.
??
gives several equivalent definitions of a
\Spin
-structure on an oriented vector bundle, including a less-used one which is most convenient for our purposes. Sections
8.2
–
8.4
discuss
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures,
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery, and
\Pin^{\pm}
-cobordism respectively. Then in
??
, we give conditions involving
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures for when integral Dehn surgery can be realised ambiently. This will be the key result in our proof of Theorem
C
.

8.1.
\Spin
-structures on vector bundles

Let
X
be a topological space. Recall that for
n\geq 2
,
\Spin(n)
is the unique connected double cover of
\SO(n)
, and
\Spin(1)
is the discrete double cover of
\SO(1)=\{1\}
. Let
\xi\colon E\to X
be an oriented rank
n
vector bundle, and let
f_{\xi}\colon X\to\BSO(n)
be its classifying map. A
\Spin
-structure
on
\xi
is a homotopy class of lifts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\BSpin(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\BSO(n).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\xi}}

The oriented bundle
\xi
admits a
\Spin
-structure if and only if
w_{2}(\xi)=0
. If
f\colon Y\to X
is any continuous map and
\mathfrak{s}
is a
\Spin
-structure on
\xi
, we write
f^{*}\mathfrak{s}
for the
\Spin
-structure on
f^{*}\xi
given by composing
f
with a lift
X\to\BSpin(n)
representing
\mathfrak{s}
.

There are many equivalent definitions of
\Spin
-structures; see Chapter IV of
[
19
]
and
[
7
]
for surveys and further details. We will require an equivalent definition in terms of compatible stable framings over loops.

Definition
\thetheorem

(
[
7
, Definition 1.3]
)
.

A
\Spin
-structure on
\xi
is a choice of stable framing of
\gamma^{*}\xi
for each continuous map
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to X
, such that for any compact surface
\Sigma
and any continuous map
f\colon\Sigma\to X
, the stable framing of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}\xi
extends to a stable framing of
f^{*}\xi
.

If
X
has a CW-structure, this definition of a
\Spin
-structure may be seen as analogous to the well-known definition of a
\Spin
-structure on
\xi
as a stable framing over the 1-skeleton of
X
which extends over the 2-skeleton. One key consequence of
??
is that
\Spin
-structures on
\xi
and
\xi\oplus\varepsilon
correspond naturally, where
\varepsilon
is the trivial line bundle over
X
.

Henceforth, we will use
??
as our primary definition of a
\Spin
-structure.

If
\xi\colon E\to X
admits a
\Spin
-structure, then the set of
\Spin
-structures on
\xi
is an
H^{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
-torsor. The action of
a\in H^{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
is given by changing the stable framing of
\gamma^{*}\xi
for a map
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to X
if and only if
\langle a,\gamma_{*}[S^{1}]\rangle=1\in{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
. See §4.2 of
[
7
]
for a slightly different description of the action in terms of
??
as well as proof that the action is free and transitive, or §1 of
[
18
]
for a more classical viewpoint.

This means that a
\Spin
-structure is determined by the stable framings of
\xi
on embedded circles representing a basis of
H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, as we spell out in the next lemma.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
\xi\colon E\to X
be an oriented rank
n
vector bundle which admits a
\Spin
-structure. Let
\Gamma
be a collection of embeddings
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to X
such that the tuple
\big(\gamma_{*}[S^{1}]\in H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\big)_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
is a basis for
H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Then the following data are equivalent:

(i)

A
\Spin
-structure on
\xi
; and

(ii)

A choice of stable framing of
\gamma^{*}\xi
for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

The implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is immediate by
??
. The fact that action of
H^{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
on
\Spin
-structures is both free and transitive implies that two
\Spin
-structures on
\xi
determine the same stable framings of
\big\{\gamma^{*}\xi\big\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
if and only if they are equivalent
\Spin
-structures.
∎

We will use
??
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) to construct
\Spin
-structures with particular properties by first choosing the appropriate stable framings over a basis of
H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
, then extending them to a
\Spin
-structure over the whole vector bundle.

8.2.
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures

Let
\xi\colon E\to X
be a rank
n
vector bundle, not necessarily orientable.
Write
\det\xi\coloneq\bigwedge^{n}\xi
for the line bundle given by the top exterior power of
\xi
. A
\Pin^{-}
-structure
on
\xi
is a
\Spin
-structure on
\xi\oplus\det\xi
. A
\Pin^{+}
-structure
on
\xi
is a
\Spin
-structure on
\xi\oplus 3\det\xi
. We refer the reader to
[
18
]
and
[
7
]
for more details and equivalent definitions.
We will often talk about a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
\xi
being a
\Spin
-structure on
\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi
, without choosing a sign or specifying that
\ell=2\pm 1
.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

To make sense of this definition, we must show that for
\ell\geq 1
odd,
\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi
is orientable and has a preferred orientation
[
7
, §2.1]
.
To see that it is orientable, it is enough to note that the line bundle
\det\xi
has the same orientation character as
\xi
, so
\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi
is orientable for any
\ell\geq 1
odd.
To see that it has a preferred orientation, choose an orientation of the fibre
\mathchoice{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{x}}{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{x}}{{\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{x}}{{\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{x}}
for each
x\in X
. This determines an orientation of
\mathchoice{{(\ell\det\xi)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{x}}{{(\ell\det\xi)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{x}}{{(\ell\det\xi)\rule[-4.76385pt]{0.0pt}{11.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.90277pt]{0.0pt}{10.44446pt}}_{x}}{{(\ell\det\xi)\rule[-4.26385pt]{0.0pt}{9.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.40277pt]{0.0pt}{8.44446pt}}_{x}}
for each
x\in X
, and for
\ell
odd, reversing the orientation of one reverses the orientation of the other. Hence the fibre
\mathchoice{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{x}}{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{x}}{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-4.76385pt]{0.0pt}{11.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.90277pt]{0.0pt}{10.44446pt}}_{x}}{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-4.26385pt]{0.0pt}{9.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.40277pt]{0.0pt}{8.44446pt}}_{x}}
has a preferred orientation for each
x\in X
. To check that these combine to give a preferred global orientation of
\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi
, note that for any open
U\subseteq X
such that
\mathchoice{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{U}}{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{U}}{{\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{U}}{{\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{U}}
is orientable, the preferred orientations of the fibres over each
x\in U
combine to give the orientation of
\mathchoice{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{U}}{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-5.51385pt]{0.0pt}{14.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.65277pt]{0.0pt}{13.44446pt}}_{U}}{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-4.76385pt]{0.0pt}{11.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.90277pt]{0.0pt}{10.44446pt}}_{U}}{{(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi)\rule[-4.26385pt]{0.0pt}{9.30554pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.40277pt]{0.0pt}{8.44446pt}}_{U}}
induced by a choice of orientation of
\mathchoice{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{U}}{{\xi\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}_{U}}{{\xi\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}_{U}}{{\xi\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}_{U}}
. There is a cover of
X
by such open neighbourhoods since
\xi
is locally trivial, so the preferred orientations of each fibre must combine to give a global orientation.

Remark
\thetheorem
.

There is also a definition of a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure in terms of lifts of classifying maps
[
18
, §1]
. For each
n\geq 1
, there exist two Lie groups
\Pin^{\pm}(n)
fitting into a central extension

1\to{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}\to\Pin^{\pm}(n)\to\mathrm{O}(n)\to 1.

A
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on a
\xi
is equivalent to a homotopy class of lifts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\BPin^{\pm}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\BO(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\xi}}

of the classifying map
f_{\xi}\colon X\to\BO(n)
.

One can show that
\xi
admits a
\Pin^{+}
-structure if and only if
w_{2}(\xi)=0
, and admits a
\Pin^{-}
-structure if and only if
w_{2}(\xi)+w_{1}^{2}(\xi)=0
. In either case, if
\xi
admits a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure, the set of
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures is naturally a
H^{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
-torsor. If
\xi
is oriented,
\det(\xi)
is trivial, so there is a natural bijection between the
\Spin
-structures,
\Pin^{-}
-structures, and
\Pin^{+}
-structures on
\xi
.

We note the following for ease of referencing.

Lemma
\thetheorem

(
[
18
, Lemma 1.6]
)
.

Let
\xi\colon E\to X
be a vector bundle, and let
\varepsilon
be a trivial bundle on
X
with a choice of stable framing. Then a choice of
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on one of
\xi
or
\varepsilon\oplus\xi
uniquely determines a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on the other.

This follows by observing that, since
\varepsilon
is trivial,

(\varepsilon\oplus\xi)\oplus\ell\det(\varepsilon\oplus\xi)=\varepsilon\oplus(\xi\oplus\ell\det\xi),

and that
\Spin
-structures correspond naturally under stabilisation. The same result is true if
\varepsilon
is stably trivial and stably framed.

We finish this subsection by discussing
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures on smooth manifolds. For a smooth manifold
Y
, a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure
on
Y
is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on the tangent bundle
TY
. A
\Pin^{\pm}
-manifold
is a smooth manifold with a choice of
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure. The following facts can all be found in
[
18
]
, but are summarised for convenience.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
Y
be a smooth manifold. Let
Z\subseteq Y
be a submanifold, and choose a stable framing of
N_{Y}Z
. Then a choice of
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
Y
determines a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on (i)
Z
; (ii)
\partial Y
; and (iii)
Y\times I
.

Proof.

For (i), note that a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
Y
restricts to one on
\mathchoice{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{Z}}{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{Z}}{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{Z}}{{TY\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{Z}}
. When taken with a stable framing of
N_{Y}Z
, this determines a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
TZ
by
??
.
Since
N_{Y}\partial Y
is the trivial line bundle over
\partial Y
, it is naturally framed, and so (ii) follows from (i) by setting
Z=\partial Y
.
For (iii), let
\pr_{Y}\colon Y\times I\to Y
be the projection map, and note that there is a natural isomorphism

\pr_{Y}^{*}(\varepsilon\oplus TY)\cong T(Y\times I),

where
\varepsilon
is the trivial line bundle on
Y
. So the result again follows by
??
.
∎

8.3.
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery

Recall that an abstract
k
-surgery on
Y
is specified by an embedded
k
-sphere
K\subset Y
and a framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
. Equivalently, it is specified by a surgery trace
Y\times I\cup h^{k+1}
, where
h^{k+1}
is a
(k+1)
-handle attached along
K\times\{1\}
. If
Y
is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-manifold, an abstract surgery is called a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery
if the corresponding
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
Y\times I
as in
??
(iii) extends over the surgery trace; equivalently, if the surgery trace is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-manifold with
Y\times\{0\}
as a
\Pin^{\pm}
-submanifold of the boundary.

Another viewpoint on this is as follows. The framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
and a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
Y
induce a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
K
by
??
(i). The core of the attaching handle has a unique
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure since it is contractible, so determines a unique
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
K
.
The abstract surgery along
K
with framing
\varphi
is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery if and only if these two
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures on
K
agree. This second viewpoint has the advantage that it highlights that whether an abstract surgery is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery or not depends only on the stable equivalence class of
\varphi
.

For
k\neq 1
, the
k
-sphere has a unique
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure, and hence every abstract
k
-surgery is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery. For
k=1
, there are two
\Pin^{\pm}
-structures on the circle, corresponding to the two stable framings of
TS^{1}
, so there is a
{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
-valued obstruction to an abstract 1-surgery being a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery.
The result of
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery inherits a unique
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure for
k\geq 1
.

8.4.
\Pin^{\pm}
-cobordism

We say that closed
n
-dimensional
\Pin^{\pm}
-manifolds
M_{0}
,
M_{1}
are
\Pin^{\pm}
-cobordant
if there exists an
(n+1)
-dimensional
\Pin^{\pm}
-manifold
W

and a
\Pin^{\pm}
-diffeomorphism
\partial W\to M_{0}\sqcup M_{1}
.
Equivalently, we say that
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are
\Pin^{\pm}
-cobordant if there exists a finite sequence of
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgeries on
M_{0}
yielding
M_{1}
. The
\Pin^{\pm}
-cobordism classes of closed
n
-manifolds form a group under disjoint union, denoted
\Omega^{\Pin^{\pm}}_{n}
.

In low dimensions, it becomes more convenient to work with
\Pin^{-}
-structures than
\Pin^{+}
-structures, since all compact surfaces and 3-manifold admit
\Pin^{-}
-structures, while not all admit
\Pin^{+}
-structures. Moreover,
\Pin^{-}
-structures on compact surfaces and 3-manifolds behave well under cobordism.

Proposition
\thetheorem

(
[
4
,
18
]
)
.

All compact surfaces admit a
\Pin^{-}
-structure. Two closed connected
\Pin^{-}
-surfaces are
\Pin^{-}
-diffeomorphic if and only if they are abstractly diffeomorphic and
\Pin^{-}
-cobordant. All compact 3-manifolds admit a
\Pin^{-}
-structure, and
\Omega_{3}^{\Pin^{-}}=0
.

Since
\Omega_{3}^{\Pin^{-}}=0
, all compact 3-manifolds with chosen
\Pin^{-}
-structures and
\Pin^{-}
-diffeomorphic boundaries are related by
\Pin^{-}
-surgeries. The following lemma shows that these can all be chosen to be 1-surgeries. This was originally shown for closed oriented 3-manifolds by Kaplan using Kirby calculus
[
15
]
; a Morse-theoretic is proof for all compact oriented 3-manifolds is given for Lemma 5.4 of
[
30
]
.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Let
M_{0}
,
M_{1}
be compact connected 3-dimensions
\Pin^{-}
-manifolds. Suppose that
\partial M_{0}
and
\partial M_{1}
are
\Pin^{-}
-diffeomorphic, and that
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are either both orientable or both non-orientable. Then there is a sequence of
\Pin^{-}
-surgeries taking
M_{0}
to
M_{1}
, all of which can be taken to be 1-surgeries.

Proof.

The case that
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are orientable is Lemma 5.4 of
[
30
]
, so we assume that
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are both non-orientable.
Fix a
\Pin^{-}
-diffeomorphism
\psi\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}
, and let
N
be the closed 3-manifold obtained by gluing

N\coloneq M_{0}\times\{0\}\mathop{\cup}_{\id\times\{0\}}\partial M_{0}\times[0,1]\mathop{\cup}_{\psi\times\{1\}}M_{1}\times\{1\}.

Note that if
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are closed, then
N
is the disjoint union
M_{0}\sqcup M_{1}
.
Since
\psi
preserves the
\Pin^{-}
-structure on the boundaries,
N
inherits a
\Pin^{-}
-structure. Then since
\Omega_{3}^{\Pin^{-}}=0
, we can find a compact connected 4-dimensional
\Pin^{-}
-manifold
W
such that
\partial W=N
as
\Pin^{-}
-manifolds. Take a Morse function
f\colon W\to[0,1]
such that
f^{-1}(\{i\})=M_{i}\times\{i\}\subseteq\partial W
for
i=0,1
, and
f(\partial M_{0}\times\{t\})=t
for
t\in[0,1]
. This gives a handle decomposition of
W
rel.
M_{0}\times\{0\}
.

We explain how to modify this handle decomposition of
W
rel.
M_{0}\times\{0\}
into one with only 2-handles. First, since
W
is connected, every 0-handle has a cancelling 1-handle, and every 4-handle has a cancelling 3-handle. Hence we can arrange that there are no 0-handles or 4-handles.
For each 1-handle in
W
, find an embedded circle
K
which is the union of the core of the 1-handle and an arc connecting the two points in the attaching sphere.
We can choose
K
such that
N_{W}K
is trivial, by taking the band sum of
K
with an orientation-reversing loop in
M_{0}\times\{0\}
if necessary. Perform 1-surgery along
K
to obtain a new 4-manifold
W^{\prime}
, which has handle decomposition with one fewer 1-handle than
W
(see
[
13
, §5.2]
– in terms of Kirby calculus, we are replacing a dotted circle with a 0-framed circle). This surgery can always be taken to be a
\Pin^{-}
-surgery, since
N_{W}K
has two framings, one of which yields
\Pin^{-}
-surgery while the other does not.

Repeating this for every 1-handle, we may arrange the handle decomposition of
W
rel.
M_{0}\times\{0\}
has no 1-handles. Turning the handle decomposition upside down, we may also arrange that it also has no 3-handles. Reinterpreting the handle decomposition of
W
as a sequence of
\Pin^{-}
-surgeries on
M_{0}
yields the result.
∎

8.5.
Ambient integral Dehn
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery

We return to ambient integral Dehn surgery as in
??
.
Let
X
be a 5-manifold and let
Y\subset X
be a properly embedded compact 3-manifold.

Recall that for an embedded circle
K\subset Y
and 2-disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
, we say that a framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
is
\Delta
-admissible if abstract surgery along
K
with framing
\varphi
can be realised ambiently over
\Delta
.
??
says that exactly one of the two stable framings of
N_{Y}K
is given by
\Delta
-admissible framings. For a fixed
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
Y
, this
\Delta
-admissible stable framing either corresponds to the stable framing inducing
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery on
K
, or the other stable framing. This is summarised in the following lemma, analogous to Lemma 5.2 of
[
30
]
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

Let
K\subset Y
be an embedded circle with
N_{Y}K
trivial, and let
\Delta
be a 2-disc spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
. Fix a
\Pin^{\pm}
-structure on
Y
. Then exactly one of the following is true.

(i)

A framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
is
\Delta
-admissible if and only if abstract surgery along
K
with framing
\varphi
is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery.

(ii)

For any framing
\varphi
of
N_{Y}K
, exactly one of the following is true: either
\varphi
is
\Delta
-admissible; or abstract surgery along
K
with framing
\varphi
is a
\Pin^{\pm}
-surgery.

A
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y\subset X
can be thought of in terms of framings of
TX
over loops in
Y
. This is a generalisation of the ideas in
[
2
]
, but for oriented 5-manifolds other than
D^{5}
.

Lemma
\thetheorem
.

A
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
is equivalent to a choice of framing of
\gamma^{*}TX
for each continuous map
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to Y
, such that for any compact surface
\Sigma
and any continuous map
f\colon\Sigma\to Y
, the framing of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}TX
extends to a framing of
f^{*}TX
.

Proof.

We first show that over loops and compact surfaces with non-empty boundary, we can identify the vector bundles
TY\oplus\det TY\oplus\varepsilon^{Y}
and
TX
, where
\varepsilon^{Y}
is the trivial line bundle over
Y
. To this end, let
M
be either
S^{1}
or a compact surface with non-empty boundary, and let
g\colon M\to Y
be a continuous map. Since
M
has the homotopy-type of a 1-complex, the rank 2 bundle
g^{*}N_{X}Y
admits a section. Hence it splits as
g^{*}N_{X}Y=\lambda\oplus\varepsilon^{M}
, where
\varepsilon^{M}
is a trivial line bundle over
M
and
\lambda
is some other line bundle.
Since
TX
is orientable, the orientation characters of
N_{X}Y
and
TY
agree. Hence the orientations characters of
\det(g^{*}N_{X}Y)
and
\det(g^{*}TY)
agree, and in particular agree with the orientation character of
\lambda
. Then since line bundles are determined by their orientation characters, we can identify

\lambda=\det(g^{*}N_{X}Y)=\det(g^{*}TY)=g^{*}(\det TY).

Therefore, we can identify

g^{*}TX=g^{*}N_{X}Y\oplus g^{*}TY=\lambda\oplus\varepsilon^{M}\oplus g^{*}TY=g^{*}(TY\oplus\det TY\oplus\varepsilon^{Y}),

as required. Finally, since
\rank(g^{*}TX)=5\geq 3
and
M
has the homotopy-type of a 1-complex, there is a bijection between the framings and the stable framings of
g^{*}TX
. Therefore a stable framing of
g^{*}(TY\oplus\det TY)
determines a stable framing of
g^{*}TX
, and hence a framing of
g^{*}TX
; and vice versa.

The lemma then follows by considering the definitions. A
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
is a choice of stable framings of
\gamma^{*}(TY\oplus\det TY)
for each map
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to Y
, which are compatible in the sense of
??
. By the argument above, this is equivalent to a choice of stable framings of
\gamma^{*}TX
for each
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to Y
, which are compatible in the sense of the statement of the lemma.
∎

Corollary
\thetheorem
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\colon S^{1}\to X
be a collection of embeddings such that the tuple
\big([\gamma_{i}(S^{1})]\in H_{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\big)_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a basis for
H_{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. Then the following data are equivalent:

(i)

A
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
; and

(ii)

A choice of framing of
\gamma_{i}^{*}TX
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
??
and
??
.
∎

Now assume that
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is cyclic and generated by a meridian to
Y
, so that every embedded circle
K\subset Y
has a null-homotopic push-off. In exactly the cases where
??
does not apply, we construct a
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
such that all abstract
\Pin^{-}
-surgeries on
Y
can be realised ambiently. This is analogous to Proposition 5.1 of
[
30
]
, although the proof given below differs substantially from the argument given there (see
??
). The same result holds for
\Pin^{+}
-structures under the assumption that
Y
admits a
\Pin^{+}
-structure. We do not require this result however, so we omit it.

Proposition
\thetheorem
.

Suppose that
X
is orientable, that
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is cyclic and generated by a meridian to
Y
, and that
\left\langle w_{2}(X\setminus Y),\sigma\right\rangle=0\text{ for all }\sigma\in H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z}).

Then
Y
admits a
\Pin^{-}
-structure such that any abstract 1-surgery which is a
\Pin^{-}
-surgery can be performed ambiently.

Proof.

Let us first unpack what is required for such a
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
to exist.
Fix an embedded circle
K\subset Y
and a 2-disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
, which exists since
K
has a null-homotopic push-off by assumption. The disc
\Delta
determines a unique framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
which extends over
\Delta
.
By
??
, a
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
also determines a framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
. If these framings agree, all ambient 1-surgeries over
\Delta
are
\Pin^{-}
-surgeries; otherwise, none are.

Therefore, given a
\Pin^{-}
-structure on
Y
, all abstract 1-surgeries which are
\Pin^{-}
-surgeries can be performed ambiently in
X
if the following holds: for each embedded curve
K\subset Y
, there is a 2-disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
such that the framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
determined by the
\Pin^{-}
-structure extends over
\Delta
. (This is more than is strictly necessary, since we need only consider
K
such that
N_{Y}K
is trivial).

We now construct such a
\Pin^{-}
-structure using
??
. Let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{n}\subset Y
be embedded circles such that the tuple
\big([K_{i}]\in H_{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})\big)_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a basis for
H_{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}})
. For each
i=1,\ldots,n
, let
\Delta_{i}
be a 2-disc spanning
K_{i}
into
X\setminus Y
, which exists since
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is generated by a meridian to
Y
. By
??
, give
Y
the
\Pin^{-}
-structure such that the determined framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K_{i}}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K_{i}}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K_{i}}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K_{i}}}
extends over
\Delta_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. For ease of referencing, we call this
\Pin^{-}
-structure
\mathfrak{p}
.

It remains to show that for all other embedded circles
K\subset Y
, there is a disc
\Delta
spanning
K
into
X\setminus Y
such that the framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}
determined by
\mathfrak{p}
extends over
\Delta
. To this end, fix an embedded circle
K\subset Y
and an orientation of
K
.
We will construct the following three things:

•

an embedding
h\colon D^{2}\to X
such that
h(D^{2})
spans
K
into
X\setminus Y
;

•

an abstract oriented surface
\Sigma
with
\partial\Sigma\cong S^{1}
; and

•

a continuous map
f\colon\Sigma\to X
such that
f(\partial\Sigma)=K
.

These will satisfy two conditions: first, that the framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}=\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}TX
determined by
\mathfrak{p}
extends over
\Sigma
; and second, that the map

f\mathop{\cup}_{K}h\colon\Sigma\mathop{\cup}_{S^{1}}D^{2}\to X

from the closed oriented surface
\Sigma\cup D^{2}
can be homotoped to an embedding in
X\setminus Y
. Then
??
and the assumption that
w_{2}(X\setminus Y)
acts trivially on
H_{2}(X\setminus Y;\mathbb{Z})
will force
(f\cup h)^{*}TX

to be trivial. Then since the framing of

\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}=\mathchoice{{h\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.24998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.3889pt}}^{*}_{S^{1}}}{{h\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.24998pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.3889pt}}^{*}_{S^{1}}}{{h\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.16664pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.30556pt}}^{*}_{S^{1}}}{{h\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.77776pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.91667pt}}^{*}_{S^{1}}}TX
determined by
\mathfrak{p}
extends over
\Sigma
, it must also extend over
\Delta\coloneq h(D^{2})
as required.

Let us first construct
\Sigma
and the continuous map
f\colon\Sigma\to X
. We will do this in several steps. First, we will find a compact orientable surface
\widehat{\Sigma}
with at least 3 boundary components, and a continuous map
f\colon\widehat{\Sigma}\to Y
. We will then cap off all but one boundary component of
\widehat{\Sigma}
with discs to obtain
\Sigma
, and extend
f
over these discs to build the required map
\Sigma\to X
.

We construct
f\colon\widehat{\Sigma}\to Y
as follows.
Without loss of generality, suppose
K_{1},\ldots,K_{n}
are ordered such that, for some
0\leq m\leq n
,

[K]=[K_{1}]+\cdots+[K_{m}]\in H_{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}).

Then, after choosing an orientation of
K_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,m
, there exists some
\beta\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
such that

[K]+[K_{1}]+\cdots+[K_{m}]+2\beta=0\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}).

Let
L\subset Y
be an oriented embedded curve such that
\beta=[L]\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, which exists since
\dim Y=3
. Then there exists an oriented surface
\widehat{\Sigma}
with
m+3
boundary components, which we label
C
,
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
,
Q_{1}
, and
Q_{2}
, as well as a continuous map
f\colon\widehat{\Sigma}\to Y
such that the restriction of
f
to each boundary component of
\widehat{\Sigma}
is an embedding, and

f(C)=K,\quad f(C_{i})=K_{i}\,\text{ for $i=1,\ldots,m$, }\quad\text{and}\quad f(Q_{1})=f(Q_{2})=L,

as oriented embedded circles.

We can now define the oriented surface
\Sigma
to be the oriented surface formed by capping off the boundary components
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m},Q_{1}
, and
Q_{2}
with discs, so that
\partial\Sigma=C
.

Next, we describe how to extend
f
to a map
f\colon\Sigma\to X
. Choose a disc
\Delta_{L}
spanning
L
into
X\setminus Y
, which again exists since
\pi_{1}(X\setminus Y)
is generated by a meridian to
Y
.
Then extend
f\colon\widehat{\Sigma}\to Y
to a map
f\colon\Sigma\to X
such that the disc capping off
C_{i}
gets mapped to
\Delta_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
, and the two discs capping off
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
both get mapped to
\Delta_{L}
. Thus
f\colon\Sigma\to X
is a continuous map from an oriented surface with
\partial\Sigma=C\cong S^{1}
such that
f(\partial\Sigma)=K
, as was required.

Next, we show that the framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{K}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{K}}=\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}TX
determined by the
\Pin^{-}
-structure
\mathfrak{p}
extends over
\Sigma
. By
??
, the framing of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}TX

extends over

, since the image

. By construction, it extends further over the discs capping off

\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{L}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{L}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{L}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{L}}=\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{Q_{j}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{Q_{j}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{Q_{j}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{Q_{j}}}TX
given by the

-structure

does not extend over the discs capping off

for

. Then we could change the framing of
\mathchoice{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{L}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{11.13885pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{10.27777pt}}_{L}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{9.08888pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{8.2278pt}}_{L}}{{TX\rule[-3.01385pt]{0.0pt}{7.72221pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{6.86113pt}}_{L}}
we are considering. This would change the framing of

for both

and

, and hence not affect the fact that the framing of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}TX
under consideration extends over
\widehat{\Sigma}
. We may therefore assume that the framing of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\widehat{\Sigma}}}TX
under consideration extends over
\Sigma
. In particular, the framing of
\mathchoice{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.9583pt]{0.0pt}{13.19443pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-4.09721pt]{0.0pt}{12.33334pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-4.37495pt]{0.0pt}{10.52774pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.51387pt]{0.0pt}{9.66666pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}{{f\rule[-3.98607pt]{0.0pt}{8.74997pt}\mkern 2.0mu\vrule width=0.60277pt\mkern 2.5mu}{\rule[-3.12498pt]{0.0pt}{7.88889pt}}^{*}_{\partial\Sigma}}TX
determined by
\mathfrak{p}
extends over

, as required.

It remains to construct the embedding
h\colon D^{2}\to X
such that
h(D^{2})
spans
K
into
X\setminus Y
, and such that map
f\cup h\colon\Sigma\cup D^{2}\to X
can be homotoped off
Y
. We first show that
f\colon\Sigma\to X
can be homotoped off

, and use this to construct the embedding
